<details><summary>Narration per scene (paste into ElevenLabs)</summary>

Scene 1 — Leadership foundations

```text
Picture your first Monday as a team lead. Yesterday you were one of the team. Today, people are looking at you slightly differently, waiting to see what you'll do. Nobody handed you a manual. Here's the good news. Leadership isn't a personality type or a job title. It's a set of behaviours you can learn and practise. In this lecture you'll learn what those behaviours are, how to adapt your style to each person and task, how trust actually gets built, and how to set a clear direction on a single page. By the end, you'll have a team charter you can share this week.
```

Scene 2 — Why it matters

```text
Why does this matter so much? Because people rarely leave a company only because of the company. Very often, they leave a manager, or they stay because of one. The way you set direction, delegate, give feedback and back people up shapes how they feel every working day. And the stakes have gone up. Teams are more distributed, work changes quickly, and many teams now use AI tools that change who does what. In that world, a leader's job is less about knowing every answer and more about creating clarity, trust and good judgement across the team.
```

Scene 3 — Management and leadership

```text
First, let's separate management from leadership, because every team needs both. Management is about planning, organising, budgeting and solving problems, so that things run predictably. Leadership is about setting direction, aligning people behind it, and motivating them through change. Here's the key idea. A team with management but no leadership runs efficiently in the wrong direction. A team with leadership but no management has energy, but misses deadlines. Most new leaders over-invest in one side, usually the one they're comfortable with. Your aim is to notice which side your team is short of right now.
```

Scene 4 — The driving instructor

```text
Here's an analogy. Think of a good driving instructor. With a nervous beginner on day one, they give clear, step-by-step instructions: mirror, signal, manoeuvre. A few weeks in, they explain why and encourage the learner when they stall. Later, they mostly stay quiet and offer the odd word of confidence. Near the test, they sit back and let the learner drive. Same instructor, four different styles, each matched to where the learner is. That's the core of situational leadership. You adjust how much direction and how much support you give, based on the person's competence and commitment for this particular task.
```

Scene 5 — Four styles

```text
So let's make that practical. Picture a grid with two dimensions: direction, meaning what, how and when, and support, meaning listening, encouragement and involving people in decisions. Directing is high direction, lower support, for someone new and keen. Coaching is high on both, for someone learning and a bit frustrated. Supporting is lower direction, high support, for someone capable but unsure. Delegating is low on both, for an expert who's motivated. Notice the phrase for this task. The same person can need directing on budgeting and delegating on client work. The classic mistake is using your favourite style for everyone.
```

Scene 6 — Trust

```text
Now trust. A practical way to think about it is three ingredients: competence, reliability and care. Competence means you know what you're doing, or you know how to find out. Reliability means you do what you say, again and again. Care means people believe you have their interests at heart, not just your own. Trust builds slowly through small actions: following up on promises, giving credit in public, admitting mistakes, keeping confidences. It's lost quickly through the opposite: blaming, taking credit, or saying one thing and doing another. Ask yourself, which of the three ingredients is weakest for me right now?
```

Scene 7 — Worked example 1: Sara and Bilal

```text
A simple worked example. Sara has just been promoted to lead a four-person customer support team. One of her team, Bilal, is brilliant with angry customers but has never touched the reporting dashboard. Sara realises she's been treating him as an expert on everything. So she splits it. On customer escalations, she delegates fully and just asks for a monthly summary. On reporting, she directs: she sits with him for two sessions, gives a checklist, and reviews his first two reports. Within a month, Bilal is coaching others on escalations and producing reports on his own. Same person, two styles, both appropriate.
```

Scene 8 — Worked example 2: Omar in Dubai (illustrative)

```text
Now a realistic business scenario, illustrative. Omar leads a twelve-person marketing team at a mid-sized retailer in Dubai. The team is busy but scattered: twenty-three active projects, and every stakeholder thinks theirs is top priority. Omar writes a one-page team charter. Purpose: grow repeat online sales profitably. Three priorities for the quarter: the loyalty programme relaunch, the Ramadan campaign, and cutting agency spend by an illustrative fifteen per cent. And, crucially, a list of what's not a priority, including two pet projects. He shares it, invites challenge, and revises it. Six weeks later the active project list is down to eleven, and cycle times are shorter.
```

Scene 9 — Setting direction

```text
Setting direction is where many leaders stay vague. Values on a poster don't help someone decide what to do on a Tuesday afternoon. What helps is a clear purpose, a small number of priorities, how you'll measure success, and, above all, what you're not going to do. That last part is the hardest and the most useful. It gives people permission to say no. And adapt to your context. In some workplaces in Pakistan, the Gulf or parts of the US, people may expect a more directive leader. In many UK or Scandinavian teams, more consultation is expected. The principle holds, but the delivery changes.
```

Scene 10 — Watch me: charter + style map

```text
Watch me do it. I'm drafting a charter for an illustrative five-person operations team. Purpose: make our clients' onboarding fast and error-free. Priorities: a new onboarding checklist, reduce handover errors, and train two people on the billing system. Not a priority this quarter: the new internal wiki. Measures: days to onboard, and errors per month. Then I fill in my style map. Priya on billing: low competence, high commitment, so I'll direct, twice a week. Then I paste an anonymised version into an AI assistant and ask it to find anything vague and three questions a sceptic would ask. It flags that fast isn't defined. I fix it.
```

Scene 11 — Common mistakes

```text
Let's cover the common mistakes. One: using your favourite style with everyone, usually delegating too early or directing too long. Two: trying to be liked instead of being trusted. Being liked is nice; being trusted is essential. Three: staying one of the team and avoiding decisions. Four: a direction statement so vague nobody could disagree with it, which means nobody can use it either. Five: never saying what's not a priority. And six: letting AI write your charter for you. It can critique and question, but the direction has to come from you and your team's real context.
```

Scene 12 — Recap and try this now

```text
Let's recap. Leadership is behaviours: setting direction, building trust, enabling people and owning results. Match direction and support to each person for each task. Build trust through competence, reliability and care. And write direction down on one page, including what you won't do. Your try this now: in the next thirty minutes, draft your team charter and your private style map using the templates in the lesson. Share the charter with your team and ask what's unclear. Next, we'll look at the behaviour that frees up the most leadership time of all: delegation.
```

</details>

### 2. Delegating effectively

Lesson `delegation` · module “Leading teams” · target 9 min · 13 scenes · 1234 words · planned 8:56 (words ≈ 8:57) · status: **to produce**

| # | Time | Sec | Narration | On screen | Visual |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | 0:00 | 40 | Here's a question worth being honest about. How many things on your to-do list right now could someone else on your team do, perhaps not exactly the way you would, but well enough? If the answer is more than two, you've found your biggest leadership opportunity. In this lecture you'll learn what to delegate, how to be explicit about levels of authority, how to run a delegation conversation step by step, and how to stop tasks bouncing back onto your desk. By the end, you'll have a delegation brief you can use tomorrow. | Delegation<br>• What to delegate<br>• Five levels of authority<br>• The delegation conversation<br>• Stop the bounce-back | Layout: a to-do list with twelve items. 0:00 list scrolls in; 0:12 items that others could do glow amber one by one; 0:30 a counter reads '7 of 12'; 0:40 four agenda chips appear. |
| 2 | 0:40 | 40 | Why does this matter? Because leaders who don't delegate become bottlenecks. Decisions queue up waiting for them, they work late, and their teams stop growing, because every stretching task stays with the boss. It's also a fairness issue. When you keep the interesting work, you deny people the experience they need to progress. So why do capable managers hold on? Usually three reasons. It's faster to do it myself. I'll lose control. And, quietly, I'll look lazy. Each of those feels true in the short term and is costly in the long term. | Why it matters<br>• Bottlenecks and burnout<br>• Teams stop growing<br>• Three excuses: faster, control, looking lazy | Layout: a funnel labelled with the leader's name. 0:00 many task balls enter and jam at the neck; 0:15 a clock and a moon icon appear; 0:30 three speech bubbles pop up with the excuses, each then stamped 'short-term'. |
| 3 | 1:20 | 40 | Here's an analogy. Think of a head chef in a busy restaurant. If the head chef insists on cooking every dish personally, the kitchen serves twelve covers a night and everyone else peels potatoes. A great head chef designs the menu, sets the standard, trains each station, and tastes as plates go out. Their impact multiplies through the brigade. Notice what the chef doesn't give away: the menu, the standard and the final check. Delegation isn't abdication. You hand over the task, but you still own the outcome and the standard. | The head chef<br>• Doesn't cook every dish<br>• Sets the menu and standard<br>• Trains each station<br>• Tastes before it goes out | Layout: a kitchen line with five stations. 0:00 only the chef station is lit, a queue of tickets grows; 0:20 all stations light up and plates flow; 0:35 the chef stands at the pass with a 'taste' icon; 0:45 caption 'delegation is not abdication'. |
| 4 | 2:00 | 40 | So what should you delegate? Tasks others can do well enough now, or with some support. Tasks that would develop someone's skills. Tasks that recur, because the time you invest in teaching pays back every month. And tasks that aren't the core of your role. What should you usually keep? Setting direction, performance ratings, sensitive people issues and anything that needs your authority or confidentiality, unless handing it over is a deliberate development step. Here's a quick test. If this task went slightly wrong, could it be fixed? If yes, it's probably delegable. | What to delegate<br>• Can be done well enough<br>• Develops someone<br>• Recurs<br>• Not your core role<br>• Keep: direction, ratings, sensitive issues | Layout: a sorting animation. 0:00 task cards drop from above; 0:10 cards slide into a 'Delegate' tray or a 'Keep' tray; 0:40 a test card appears: 'If it went slightly wrong, could it be fixed?' with a green tick. |
| 5 | 2:40 | 43 | Here's the key idea of this lecture. Delegation isn't all or nothing. There are levels, and most frustration comes from not saying which one you mean. Level one: look into it and report back, I'll decide. Level two: recommend a course of action, I'll decide. Level three: decide, but tell me before you act. Level four: decide and act, then tell me. Level five: it's yours, no need to report back. If you expected a recommendation and got a signed contract, or they expected authority and got overruled, the problem wasn't the person. It was the missing level. | Five levels of authority<br>1 Look into it<br>2 Recommend<br>3 Decide, tell me first<br>4 Decide, tell me after<br>5 Own it | Layout: a five-step staircase. 0:00 steps appear one at a time with labels; 0:35 two avatars stand on different steps with a red lightning bolt between them captioned 'mismatch'; 0:50 they align on step three. |
| 6 | 3:23 | 40 | Now the conversation itself, step by step. First, explain the task and why it matters, because context helps people make good judgements. Second, define what done looks like, including quality and deadline. Third, agree the authority level. Fourth, clarify resources and constraints: budget, people, data, and which AI tools are approved. Fifth, agree check-ins proportionate to experience and risk. Sixth, ask them to summarise it back in their own words. That one step catches most misunderstandings. And seventh, follow up as agreed, and resist taking the task back at the first wobble. | The delegation conversation<br>• Why it matters<br>• What done looks like<br>• Authority level<br>• Resources and constraints<br>• Check-ins<br>• Summarise back<br>• Follow up | Layout: a vertical numbered timeline with seven nodes lighting in sequence every six seconds; node six 'Summarise back' pulses with a speech bubble 'So what I heard is...'; 0:45 a hand reaching to grab a task is blocked by a 'resist' shield. |
| 7 | 4:03 | 43 | A simple worked example. Jess runs a small design studio in Manchester. Every month she spends a day compiling client invoices from timesheets. She delegates it to Leo, a junior who's organised and keen. She explains why it matters: cash flow depends on it. Done looks like: invoices drafted by the third working day, matching timesheets. Level three: Leo prepares them, Jess approves before they're sent. Two check-ins in month one. Leo summarises it back and spots that two clients need purchase order numbers. By month three, Jess moves him to level four. She's got a day a month back. | Worked example 1: Jess and Leo (illustrative)<br>• Monthly invoicing<br>• Level 3, then level 4<br>• Summary-back catches PO numbers<br>• One day a month freed | Layout: a calendar month view. 0:00 one full day is blocked in red 'invoicing'; 0:15 a delegation brief card slides over; 0:30 a speech bubble 'these two need PO numbers'; 0:45 the level badge changes from 3 to 4; 0:55 the red day turns green 'Jess: strategy'. |
| 8 | 4:46 | 45 | Now a realistic business scenario, illustrative. Faisal is operations manager at a logistics firm in Riyadh, managing eight team leads. He's become the bottleneck for supplier approvals: every purchase over an illustrative five thousand riyals comes to him, and the queue is two weeks long. He sets clear levels. Under twenty thousand riyals with an approved supplier, team leads decide at level four and log it. New suppliers: level two, recommend with three quotes. Above the threshold: level three. He also writes down what he won't do: re-open decisions made within the rules. Within a quarter, the approval queue drops to two days. | Worked example 2: Faisal in Riyadh (illustrative)<br>• Approvals bottleneck: 2 weeks<br>• Levels by value and supplier type<br>• 'I won't re-open decisions made within the rules'<br>• Queue: 2 weeks → 2 days | Layout: a queue of approval tickets stacked at a manager's desk. 0:00 stack grows; 0:15 a rules table appears with three rows by value and supplier type; 0:35 tickets redirect to team-lead desks; 0:50 a counter animates '14 days' down to '2 days'. |
| 9 | 5:31 | 39 | Let's talk about the bounce-back. There's a classic idea, sometimes called monkey management. A team member walks in with a problem, the monkey, and walks out without it, because you said, leave it with me, I'll have a think. Now it's on your back. Do that five times a day and you're carrying the whole zoo. Instead, ask: what do you recommend? What options have you considered? Then agree the next step, and who owns it. The monkey stays with them, and you stay available to help, not to rescue. | Monkey management<br>• 'Leave it with me' = monkey moves to you<br>• Ask: 'What do you recommend?'<br>• Agree next step and owner<br>• Help, don't rescue | Layout: a cartoon monkey sits on a team member's shoulder. 0:00 they walk into the leader's office; 0:10 the monkey jumps to the leader when 'leave it with me' appears; 0:25 five monkeys pile up; 0:40 rewind: the question 'What do you recommend?' keeps the monkey with the team member. |
| 10 | 6:10 | 44 | Watch me do it. I'm delegating the quarterly client survey to an illustrative colleague called Hina. I open the delegation brief. Task: run the survey and summarise results. <details><summary>Narration per scene (paste into ElevenLabs)</summary>

Scene 1 — Delegation

```text
Here's a question worth being honest about. How many things on your to-do list right now could someone else on your team do, perhaps not exactly the way you would, but well enough? If the answer is more than two, you've found your biggest leadership opportunity. In this lecture you'll learn what to delegate, how to be explicit about levels of authority, how to run a delegation conversation step by step, and how to stop tasks bouncing back onto your desk. By the end, you'll have a delegation brief you can use tomorrow.
```

Scene 2 — Why it matters

```text
Why does this matter? Because leaders who don't delegate become bottlenecks. Decisions queue up waiting for them, they work late, and their teams stop growing, because every stretching task stays with the boss. It's also a fairness issue. When you keep the interesting work, you deny people the experience they need to progress. So why do capable managers hold on? Usually three reasons. It's faster to do it myself. I'll lose control. And, quietly, I'll look lazy. Each of those feels true in the short term and is costly in the long term.
```

Scene 3 — The head chef

```text
Here's an analogy. Think of a head chef in a busy restaurant. If the head chef insists on cooking every dish personally, the kitchen serves twelve covers a night and everyone else peels potatoes. A great head chef designs the menu, sets the standard, trains each station, and tastes as plates go out. Their impact multiplies through the brigade. Notice what the chef doesn't give away: the menu, the standard and the final check. Delegation isn't abdication. You hand over the task, but you still own the outcome and the standard.
```

Scene 4 — What to delegate

```text
So what should you delegate? Tasks others can do well enough now, or with some support. Tasks that would develop someone's skills. Tasks that recur, because the time you invest in teaching pays back every month. And tasks that aren't the core of your role. What should you usually keep? Setting direction, performance ratings, sensitive people issues and anything that needs your authority or confidentiality, unless handing it over is a deliberate development step. Here's a quick test. If this task went slightly wrong, could it be fixed? If yes, it's probably delegable.
```

Scene 5 — Five levels of authority

```text
Here's the key idea of this lecture. Delegation isn't all or nothing. There are levels, and most frustration comes from not saying which one you mean. Level one: look into it and report back, I'll decide. Level two: recommend a course of action, I'll decide. Level three: decide, but tell me before you act. Level four: decide and act, then tell me. Level five: it's yours, no need to report back. If you expected a recommendation and got a signed contract, or they expected authority and got overruled, the problem wasn't the person. It was the missing level.
```

Scene 6 — The delegation conversation

```text
Now the conversation itself, step by step. First, explain the task and why it matters, because context helps people make good judgements. Second, define what done looks like, including quality and deadline. Third, agree the authority level. Fourth, clarify resources and constraints: budget, people, data, and which AI tools are approved. Fifth, agree check-ins proportionate to experience and risk. Sixth, ask them to summarise it back in their own words. That one step catches most misunderstandings. And seventh, follow up as agreed, and resist taking the task back at the first wobble.
```

Scene 7 — Worked example 1: Jess and Leo (illustrative)

```text
A simple worked example. Jess runs a small design studio in Manchester. Every month she spends a day compiling client invoices from timesheets. She delegates it to Leo, a junior who's organised and keen. She explains why it matters: cash flow depends on it. Done looks like: invoices drafted by the third working day, matching timesheets. Level three: Leo prepares them, Jess approves before they're sent. Two check-ins in month one. Leo summarises it back and spots that two clients need purchase order numbers. By month three, Jess moves him to level four. She's got a day a month back.
```

Scene 8 — Worked example 2: Faisal in Riyadh (illustrative)

```text
Now a realistic business scenario, illustrative. Faisal is operations manager at a logistics firm in Riyadh, managing eight team leads. He's become the bottleneck for supplier approvals: every purchase over an illustrative five thousand riyals comes to him, and the queue is two weeks long. He sets clear levels. Under twenty thousand riyals with an approved supplier, team leads decide at level four and log it. New suppliers: level two, recommend with three quotes. Above the threshold: level three. He also writes down what he won't do: re-open decisions made within the rules. Within a quarter, the approval queue drops to two days.
```

Scene 9 — Monkey management

```text
Let's talk about the bounce-back. There's a classic idea, sometimes called monkey management. A team member walks in with a problem, the monkey, and walks out without it, because you said, leave it with me, I'll have a think. Now it's on your back. Do that five times a day and you're carrying the whole zoo. Instead, ask: what do you recommend? What options have you considered? Then agree the next step, and who owns it. The monkey stays with them, and you stay available to help, not to rescue.
```

Scene 10 — Watch me: the delegation brief

```text
Watch me do it. I'm delegating the quarterly client survey to an illustrative colleague called Hina. I open the delegation brief. Task: run the survey and summarise results. Why: leadership uses it to decide where to invest in service. Done: a four-page summary by the fifteenth. Level three: she decides the questions, shows me before sending. Resources: last quarter's survey and our survey tool. Constraint: no client names pasted into public AI tools. Check-ins on the fifth and twelfth. Then I add a row to my tracker, and in the last column I write, what I won't do: rewrite her questions.
```

Scene 11 — Common mistakes

```text
Common mistakes. Delegating only boring tasks, so people never grow. Dumping a task without context, then being disappointed. Leaving the authority level vague. Hovering and micromanaging, or the opposite: disappearing until the deadline. Taking the task back at the first difficulty. Forgetting that you remain accountable, so blaming the person publicly when it goes wrong. And a new one: delegating to someone who'll rely on an AI tool, without saying what data can't go into it or reminding them that they own the checking. Delegate the task, never the judgement about quality.
```

Scene 12 — Is it working?

```text
How do you know your delegation is working? Look for four signals. First, your calendar: are you spending more of your week on direction, coaching and the few decisions only you can make? Second, cycle time: are decisions and approvals moving faster? Third, growth: are people handling tasks at higher authority levels than three months ago? And fourth, bounce-backs: how many tasks came back to you this month? Keep a simple tally for a few weeks. If the tally isn't falling, look at your own behaviour first. Are you really leaving the monkey where it belongs?
```

Scene 13 — Recap and try this now

```text
Let's recap. Delegation multiplies your impact and develops your people. Choose tasks that others can do, that build skills and that recur. Always name the authority level. Run the seven-step conversation, including the summary back. Keep the monkey with its owner. And remember you're still accountable for the outcome. Your try this now: pick one recurring task you do every week, fill in the delegation brief from the lesson, and have the conversation within the next five working days. Next, we'll look at motivating and developing the people you've just delegated to.
```

</details>

<details><summary>Narration per scene (paste into ElevenLabs)</summary>

Scene 1 — Motivating and developing people

```text
Think about the best manager you've ever had. Now think about what they actually did. It's rarely a big speech. More often it was small things: they trusted you with something important, they noticed when you'd improved, they had your back in a difficult meeting, and they helped you see where you could go next. In this lecture you'll learn what research suggests motivates people at work, how to run one-to-ones that people value, how to coach with the GROW model, and how to plan development so people actually grow.
```

Scene 2 — Why it matters

```text
Why does this matter? Because motivated, growing people do better work, stay longer, and help others grow too. And disengagement is expensive. It shows up as quiet quitting, missed ideas and good people leaving. You can't install motivation in someone like software. But you can create the conditions where it's much more likely. And you can do that with habits that take an hour or two a week, not a big programme. That's what we'll build today.
```

Scene 3 — What motivates

```text
First, the concept. Pay and security matter a great deal. If people feel unfairly paid or insecure, motivation suffers. But beyond a fair baseline, research on motivation, for example self-determination theory, points to three psychological needs. Autonomy: some control over how, when and with whom I work. Competence, or mastery: the sense that I'm getting better and doing good work. And relatedness: belonging, and a connection to something that matters. Here's the key idea. Your job is to design work and conversations that feed those three needs.
```

Scene 4 — The game designer

```text
Here's an analogy. Think of a video game that people can't put down. It gives you choices about how to play: autonomy. It gets steadily harder at just the right pace, and shows you your progress: mastery. And often you play with friends, towards a goal in the story: belonging and purpose. Nobody has to bribe players to keep going. Good work can have the same design. Clear goals, room to choose the route, visible progress, and people you enjoy working with. You're the level designer.
```

Scene 5 — One-to-ones

```text
So how do you do that in practice? The single most effective habit is a regular one-to-one. Thirty minutes every week or two. And here's the rule: it's their meeting, not your status update. Their topics first. Then blockers: what can I unblock for you? Then feedback both ways, including asking, what's one thing I could do differently to help you? Then, at least monthly, growth: skills, career goals and stretch opportunities. Finish with clear actions. And keep brief notes. Nothing destroys trust faster than a promise you forgot.
```

Scene 6 — Coaching with GROW

```text
Now coaching. When someone brings you a problem, the fastest thing is to tell them the answer. But people remember what they discover, and they build capability. So use GROW. Goal: what would a good outcome look like? Reality: what's happening now, and what have you tried? Options: what are three possible approaches, and what else? Will, or way forward: which will you take, what's the first step, and when? A useful personal rule is to ask at least three questions before you offer a suggestion. It feels slow. It saves enormous time later.
```

Scene 7 — Worked example 1: Maria and Kamran (illustrative)

```text
A simple worked example. Maria, a developer in a small software team in Lahore, tells her lead, Kamran, I'm stuck, the client keeps changing the requirements. Kamran's instinct is to call the client himself. Instead, he coaches. Goal? A stable scope for the next sprint. Reality? Changes arrive by chat, from three different people. Options? Maria suggests a single change log, one named contact, and a weekly change review. Will? She'll propose it to the client on Thursday. It works, and next time she handles it without asking.
```

Scene 8 — Worked example 2: Claire in London (illustrative)

```text
Now a realistic business scenario, illustrative. Claire leads a fifteen-person customer success team at a software company in London. In the annual survey, growth scores are low and two strong people have left for bigger roles elsewhere. Claire introduces three things. Fortnightly one-to-ones with a growth slot every month. A one-line development plan for everyone: career goal, skill, stretch task, mentor, and one piece of learning. And specific, public recognition in the weekly team call. Six months later, two people have moved into internal team-lead roles, and the growth score has risen noticeably. The investment: about three hours of Claire's week.
```

Scene 9 — Development and recognition

```text
A word on development and recognition. Most learning happens on the job. A widely used rule of thumb says it comes mainly from challenging experiences, then from people, like coaching and mentoring, and least from formal courses. Treat the exact proportions as a rough guide, not a law. So when you plan development, lead with a stretch assignment. And recognition should be specific, timely and sincere. Not great job everyone. Instead: the way you restructured the onboarding email cut our support tickets this week. Thank you. That tells the person exactly what to do more of.
```

Scene 10 — Watch me: preparing a 1:1

```text
Watch me do it. I'm preparing for a one-to-one with an illustrative team member, Tom. I open his shared one-to-one doc. Under their topics, he's added: unsure about the new pricing process. I note one piece of feedback in situation, behaviour, impact form. Under growth, I check his plan: goal, account management; stretch task, lead the renewal call with a mid-sized client. I also ask an AI assistant, using no personal details, for five coaching questions on building confidence in client calls. I pick two that fit, and write my rule at the top: three questions before advice.
```

Scene 11 — Common mistakes

```text
Common mistakes. Cancelling one-to-ones when you're busy, which tells people exactly where they rank. Turning them into status updates. Giving answers instead of coaching. Assuming everyone is motivated by what motivates you. Development plans that list courses and nothing else. Generic praise, or praise only for the loudest people. And letting an AI write recognition or performance feedback you don't genuinely mean. People can tell, and it corrodes trust. Use AI for preparation, never as a substitute for your own attention.
```

Scene 12 — Measuring success

```text
How do you know it's working? Measure it, gently. Track whether one-to-ones actually happen: aim for well over nine in ten kept, not moved. Watch your team's engagement or pulse survey, especially the questions about growth and recognition. Count internal moves and promotions, not just exits. And ask directly, every quarter: on a scale of one to ten, how much are you learning here, and what would make it a ten? Write the answers down. If the same theme appears from three people, that's not a personal issue. That's a system issue, and it's yours to fix.
```

Scene 13 — Recap and try this now

```text
Let's recap. Beyond fair pay, people are motivated by autonomy, mastery and belonging with purpose. One-to-ones are their meeting. Coach with GROW, and ask before you tell. Plan development around stretch experiences, and recognise specific contributions sincerely. Your try this now: set up a shared one-to-one doc for each person using the lesson template, book the next meeting, and write one development line for each person on your team. Next module, we move to feedback, difficult conversations and conflict, starting with how to give feedback people can actually use.
```

</details>

<details><summary>Narration per scene (paste into ElevenLabs)</summary>

Scene 1 — Giving feedback

```text
Here's a sentence that makes almost everyone's stomach drop: can I give you some feedback? Why does it feel like a threat, when feedback is how we all improve? Usually because most feedback we've received has been vague, late or about us as people rather than about what we did. In this lecture you'll learn the SBI model, the difference between reinforcing and redirecting feedback, how to time and deliver it, how to adapt it across cultures, and how to give feedback upward and to peers. By the end, you'll have a draft you can deliver this week.
```

Scene 2 — Why it matters

```text
Why does this matter? Because without feedback, people are guessing. Good performers don't know what to repeat, and struggling performers don't know what to change until it's a formal problem, which is unfair to them. Feedback is also how you shape culture. What you notice and comment on tells the team what really matters, whatever the values poster says. And the cost of avoiding a small piece of feedback today is usually a much harder conversation in three months' time.
```

Scene 3 — The SBI model

```text
Here's the key idea: feedback should describe what someone did and what effect it had, not who they are. The SBI model, developed at the Center for Creative Leadership, gives you a structure. Situation: when and where, specifically. Behaviour: what the person actually did or said, observable, the kind of thing a camera could record. Impact: the effect it had on you, the team, the client or the work. Then, crucially, invite their view. Situation, behaviour, impact, and a question. That's it.
```

Scene 4 — The sat nav

```text
Here's an analogy. Think about a sat nav. It doesn't say, you're a bad driver. It says: in two hundred metres, you missed the turning, recalculating route. Situation, behaviour, impact, and a way forward. It's specific, it's neutral, and it's immediate. That's why you trust it and act on it. Now imagine a sat nav that stayed silent for three hours and then said, frankly, your driving this afternoon has been disappointing. Useless. Good feedback works like a good sat nav: timely, specific, and about the route, not the driver.
```

Scene 5 — Reinforcing and redirecting

```text
There are two kinds of feedback, and you need both. Reinforcing feedback tells someone what to keep doing. Redirecting feedback tells them what to change. Many leaders only give the second, so every one-to-one feels like a dressing down. Aim to give much more reinforcing than redirecting feedback, and make the reinforcing kind just as specific. Great work is pleasant but tells nobody anything. And a note on the old feedback sandwich, praise, criticism, praise. People learn to wait for the but, and the praise stops meaning anything. Keep them separate and honest.
```

Scene 6 — Timing and setting

```text
Timing and setting matter as much as the words. Give feedback soon, ideally within a day or two, while the details are fresh. Reinforce in public when your culture welcomes it, and redirect in private, always. Choose a moment when the person can actually listen, not two minutes before they present to a client. Check your own state too. If you're angry, wait until you can be curious. And here's the step people skip: after SBI, ask a question. What was your thinking there? You may discover context that changes everything.
```

Scene 7 — Worked example 1: Nadia and Hassan (illustrative)

```text
A simple worked example. Nadia manages a small events team in Karachi. Her colleague, Hassan, keeps sending client updates late in the evening, full of typos. Her first instinct is to say, you need to be more professional. Instead she uses SBI. In yesterday's update to the hotel client, which went out at eleven at night, there were three errors in the dates. The client called me this morning worried about the booking. Then she asks: what's going on with timing on those updates? Hassan explains he's covering two roles. Now they fix the real problem: workload.
```

Scene 8 — Worked example 2: Reem and Yusuf in Riyadh (illustrative)

```text
Now a realistic business scenario, illustrative. At an agency in Riyadh, account director Reem notices that her senior strategist, Yusuf, has pushed back on a client's brief in front of the whole client team for the second time. The client's marketing head later tells Reem she felt embarrassed. Reem books a private conversation. Situation: in Monday's workshop with the client's marketing team. Behaviour: you said their brief was fundamentally wrong, in front of her team. Impact: she told me she felt embarrassed, and she's now asking for a different strategist. Then Reem asks for his view, and they agree how he'll raise disagreement privately first.
```

Scene 9 — Feedback across cultures

```text
Culture shapes how feedback lands. In some cultures, direct negative feedback is normal and valued. In others, including many workplaces in South Asia, the Gulf and parts of East Asia, it's more often given indirectly, privately and with care for face. Neither is right. What matters is that you're understood and that the person can act. So adjust the wrapping: more context, more private, more softening words where needed, but keep the behaviour and impact specific. Feedback upward and to peers uses the same model. Ask permission first: would it help if I shared an observation about Tuesday's meeting?
```

Scene 10 — Watch me: SBI builder

```text
Watch me do it. I'm preparing reinforcing feedback for an illustrative analyst, Imran. I open the SBI builder. Situation: Thursday's quarterly review with the finance team. Behaviour: you opened with the one-line answer, then showed only three charts. Impact: the CFO approved the budget change in the meeting, which normally takes two rounds. Then a question: how did you decide what to cut? I paste the anonymised draft into an AI assistant and ask it to flag any judgement words. It flags nothing on this one. Then I log it in my feedback log, and I notice Imran hasn't had redirecting feedback in months. Is that fair to him?
```

Scene 11 — Measuring success

```text
Measuring success is simple. Does behaviour change? Do people start to ask you for feedback? Is your log balanced across the team and between reinforcing and redirecting? And the hard one: ask for feedback on your feedback. Once a quarter, ask each person, how useful is the feedback you get from me, and how could it be more useful? Notice your reaction when you receive it. If you get defensive, your team will learn that feedback flows only one way. Model receiving well, by saying thank you and asking a follow-up question.
```

Scene 12 — Common mistakes

```text
Common mistakes. Labels instead of behaviour: you're careless, you're not a team player. Waiting for the annual review. Only redirecting, never reinforcing. The feedback sandwich. Giving feedback in public that should be private. Talking at someone instead of asking their view. Softening so much that the message disappears. And a new one: sending AI-polished feedback by chat to avoid a conversation. Written feedback on something sensitive, without a conversation, almost always lands worse than you intend.
```

Scene 13 — Recap and try this now

```text
Let's recap. Feedback describes behaviour and impact, not personality. Use SBI, then ask for their view. Give far more reinforcing than redirecting feedback, and keep them separate. Give it soon, privately when redirecting, adapted to culture but still specific. Your try this now: pick one person, draft one reinforcing piece of feedback using the SBI builder in the lesson, and deliver it within forty-eight hours. Then start your feedback log. Next, we'll take on the conversations everyone avoids: the genuinely difficult ones.
```

</details>

### 5. Difficult conversations and receiving feedback

Lesson `difficult-conversations` · module “Feedback, difficult conversations and conflict” · target 8 min · 13 scenes · 1185 words · planned 8:35 (words ≈ 8:35) · status: **to produce**

| 1 | 0:00 | 42 | Is there a conversation you've been putting off? Perhaps with a colleague who keeps missing deadlines, a manager who took credit for your work, or a client who's become unreasonable. Most of us have at least one. And the longer we wait, the heavier it gets. In this lecture you'll learn why some conversations feel so hard, the three layers underneath every difficult conversation, a step-by-step way to prepare, what to do in the room, and how to deliver bad news with dignity. By the end, you'll have a plan for the conversation you've been avoiding. | Difficult conversations<br>• Why they feel hard<br>• Three layers<br>• Prepare step by step<br>• In the room<br>• Bad news with dignity | Layout: a closed door with a calendar on it where a meeting keeps getting dragged to next week. 0:00 the drag repeats three times; 0:15 a weight icon on the door grows heavier; 0:30 the door opens slightly to reveal five agenda chips. |
| 2 | 0:42 | 37 | Why do these conversations matter so much? Because avoided conversations don't disappear. They leak. They show up as frustration, gossip, workarounds and eventually good people leaving. And when difficult conversations finally happen late, they tend to happen badly: angry, rushed or by email. Leaders who can have hard conversations early and calmly build teams where problems surface quickly. That's a real performance advantage. It's also a matter of fairness. The person deserves to know there's an issue while they can still do something about it. | Why it matters<br>• Avoided issues leak<br>• Late = angry, rushed, email<br>• Early and calm = problems surface<br>• Fairness: time to act | Layout: a pipe with a small crack. 0:00 drips labelled frustration, gossip, workarounds fall into a bucket; 0:20 the bucket overflows labelled 'resignation'; 0:35 rewind: a hand seals the crack early with a 'conversation' patch. |
| 3 | 1:19 | 38 | Here's the concept. Research from the Harvard Negotiation Project, in the book Difficult Conversations by Douglas Stone, Bruce Patton and Sheila Heen, suggests every difficult conversation is really three conversations at once. The what happened conversation: who's right, who meant what, who's to blame. The feelings conversation: the emotions sitting under the surface, often unspoken. And the identity conversation: what this says about me. Am I competent? Am I a good person? Here's the key idea. You can't resolve the first layer while the other two are ignored. | Three conversations<br>• What happened<br>• Feelings<br>• Identity<br>• Stone, Patton and Heen, Difficult Conversations | Layout: three stacked transparent layers. 0:00 top layer 'What happened' with two arguing arrows; 0:15 middle layer 'Feelings' with emotion icons; 0:30 bottom layer 'Identity' with a mirror icon; 0:45 a book icon labelled with the three authors. |
| 4 | 1:57 | 42 | Here's an analogy. Think of a stone dropped into a still pond. The stone is the event: the missed deadline, the curt email. But what you actually deal with in the conversation are the ripples: hurt, embarrassment, worry about reputation. If you only talk about the stone, you'll keep wondering why the water won't settle. Good difficult conversations acknowledge the ripples. Not with a therapy session, but with a sentence or two. I imagine this has been frustrating for you too. That small acknowledgement often lets the water go calm enough to see the stone clearly. | The stone and the ripples<br>• The stone: the event<br>• The ripples: feelings and identity<br>• Acknowledge the ripples<br>• Then the event becomes clear | Layout: a top-down pond. 0:00 a stone labelled 'missed deadline' drops; 0:08 ripples spread labelled 'hurt', 'embarrassed', 'worried'; 0:25 a text bubble 'I imagine this has been frustrating' appears and the ripples calm; 0:40 the stone becomes clearly visible. |
| 5 | 2:39 | 43 | So how do you prepare? Step one, purpose. What outcome do you want for them, for you and for the relationship? If the honest answer is to make them feel bad, pause. Step two, map both stories of what happened, including what you don't know. Step three, name the feelings, yours and theirs. Step four, spot the identity threat: what feels at stake for each of you? Step five, write a neutral opening, what the book calls the third story, the version a fair observer would tell. And step six, decide your next step if you don't agree. | Preparing<br>1 Purpose<br>2 Both stories + unknowns<br>3 Feelings<br>4 Identity threat<br>5 Neutral opening<br>6 Your next step if no agreement | Layout: a six-step checklist on a clipboard. 0:00 each step ticks in sequence every eight seconds; 0:30 step five expands to show a third-person observer icon between two perspectives; 0:50 step six shows a small fork in a path. |
| 6 | 3:22 | 41 | In the room, three moves do most of the work. First, open with your neutral third story, briefly, in under thirty seconds. Something like, I'd like to talk about how handovers have been going. I see it one way and I suspect you see it differently. I'd like to understand your view. Second, listen to learn, not to rebut. Ask, paraphrase, and acknowledge feelings. Third, share your view as your view: the facts you observed, the impact, and what you'd like. Then problem-solve together. If emotions run high, it's fine to pause and come back. | In the room<br>• Open with the third story<br>• Listen to learn<br>• Share your view as your view<br>• Solve together<br>• Pausing is allowed | Layout: a three-panel storyboard. 0:00 panel one, a short speech bubble with a stopwatch at 0:28; 0:15 panel two, a listening ear with paraphrase bubbles; 0:30 panel three, a facts card and a request card; 0:45 a shared whiteboard between two figures. |
| 7 | 4:03 | 40 | A simple worked example. Aaron and Lucy co-run a small bakery in Leeds. Lucy feels Aaron always leaves the early shift to her. Aaron thinks Lucy prefers it. For weeks, tension builds. Lucy prepares. Purpose: a fair rota and no resentment. Feelings: she feels taken for granted. Identity: she's worried that raising it will make her seem petty. She opens with, I'd like to talk about the rota. I think we see it differently, and I'd like to understand how it looks to you. Aaron genuinely hadn't realised. They agree to alternate weeks. | Worked example 1: a bakery in Leeds (illustrative)<br>• Issue: the early shift<br>• Hidden feeling: taken for granted<br>• Identity: 'I'll seem petty'<br>• Third-story opening<br>• Outcome: alternate weeks | Layout: a rota calendar with early shifts all highlighted under one name. 0:00 highlights glow; 0:15 a thought bubble 'taken for granted'; 0:30 an opening-line card; 0:45 the rota re-colours into alternating weeks. |
| 8 | 4:43 | 42 | Now a realistic business scenario, illustrative. Sameer leads a product team at a fintech in Islamabad. | 9 | 5:25 | 38 | Delivering bad news is its own skill: redundancy, a rejected promotion, a cancelled project. Say it early in the conversation, clearly, in the first minute. Explain what happens next and what support exists. Don't argue with emotions, and don't say, I know how you feel. | 11 | 6:47 | 37 | How do you know it went well? Not whether it was comfortable, because it rarely is. Ask instead: did we both understand each other's view? Send a short follow-up note the same day summarising what you agreed. And book a check-in. Many difficult conversations need a second, shorter conversation once both people have had time to think. | Did it work?<br>• Mutual understanding<br>• Specific next step, owner, date<br>• Relationship intact<br>• Change over weeks<br>• Follow-up note + check-in | Layout: a checklist on the left ticks four items; 0:30 an email thumbnail titled 'What we agreed today' slides in; 0:40 a calendar check-in appears two weeks later. |
| 12 | 7:24 | 33 | Common mistakes. Waiting until you're angry. Rehearsing only your argument, not their story. Opening with a verdict: we need to talk about your attitude. Sending a long message instead of meeting. Burying bad news. Arguing with feelings. Trying to win the what happened conversation and losing the relationship. And using an AI role-play as a prediction of what the other person will say. <details><summary>Narration per scene (paste into ElevenLabs)</summary>

Scene 1 — Difficult conversations

```text
Is there a conversation you've been putting off? Perhaps with a colleague who keeps missing deadlines, a manager who took credit for your work, or a client who's become unreasonable. Most of us have at least one. And the longer we wait, the heavier it gets. In this lecture you'll learn why some conversations feel so hard, the three layers underneath every difficult conversation, a step-by-step way to prepare, what to do in the room, and how to deliver bad news with dignity. By the end, you'll have a plan for the conversation you've been avoiding.
```

Scene 2 — Why it matters

```text
Why do these conversations matter so much? Because avoided conversations don't disappear. They leak. They show up as frustration, gossip, workarounds and eventually good people leaving. And when difficult conversations finally happen late, they tend to happen badly: angry, rushed or by email. Leaders who can have hard conversations early and calmly build teams where problems surface quickly. That's a real performance advantage. It's also a matter of fairness. The person deserves to know there's an issue while they can still do something about it.
```

Scene 3 — Three conversations

```text
Here's the concept. Research from the Harvard Negotiation Project, in the book Difficult Conversations by Douglas Stone, Bruce Patton and Sheila Heen, suggests every difficult conversation is really three conversations at once. The what happened conversation: who's right, who meant what, who's to blame. The feelings conversation: the emotions sitting under the surface, often unspoken. And the identity conversation: what this says about me. Am I competent? Am I a good person? Here's the key idea. You can't resolve the first layer while the other two are ignored.
```

Scene 4 — The stone and the ripples

```text
Here's an analogy. Think of a stone dropped into a still pond. The stone is the event: the missed deadline, the curt email. But what you actually deal with in the conversation are the ripples: hurt, embarrassment, worry about reputation. If you only talk about the stone, you'll keep wondering why the water won't settle. Good difficult conversations acknowledge the ripples. Not with a therapy session, but with a sentence or two. I imagine this has been frustrating for you too. That small acknowledgement often lets the water go calm enough to see the stone clearly.
```

Scene 5 — Preparing

```text
So how do you prepare? Step one, purpose. What outcome do you want for them, for you and for the relationship? If the honest answer is to make them feel bad, pause. Step two, map both stories of what happened, including what you don't know. Step three, name the feelings, yours and theirs. Step four, spot the identity threat: what feels at stake for each of you? Step five, write a neutral opening, what the book calls the third story, the version a fair observer would tell. And step six, decide your next step if you don't agree.
```

Scene 6 — In the room

```text
In the room, three moves do most of the work. First, open with your neutral third story, briefly, in under thirty seconds. Something like, I'd like to talk about how handovers have been going. I see it one way and I suspect you see it differently. I'd like to understand your view. Second, listen to learn, not to rebut. Ask, paraphrase, and acknowledge feelings. Third, share your view as your view: the facts you observed, the impact, and what you'd like. Then problem-solve together. If emotions run high, it's fine to pause and come back.
```

Scene 7 — Worked example 1: a bakery in Leeds (illustrative)

```text
A simple worked example. Aaron and Lucy co-run a small bakery in Leeds. Lucy feels Aaron always leaves the early shift to her. Aaron thinks Lucy prefers it. For weeks, tension builds. Lucy prepares. Purpose: a fair rota and no resentment. Feelings: she feels taken for granted. Identity: she's worried that raising it will make her seem petty. She opens with, I'd like to talk about the rota. I think we see it differently, and I'd like to understand how it looks to you. Aaron genuinely hadn't realised. They agree to alternate weeks.
```

Scene 8 — Worked example 2: Sameer and Zainab (illustrative)

```text
Now a realistic business scenario, illustrative. Sameer leads a product team at a fintech in Islamabad. A senior engineer, Zainab, has missed three sprint commitments, and her code reviews have become short and sharp. Sameer prepares. His story: she's disengaged. Unknowns: what's changed for her? Feelings: he's frustrated, she may be embarrassed. Identity: she's the team's most respected engineer. His opening is neutral. In the conversation, Zainab reveals she's been pulled into a regulatory project by another director and hasn't said no. The real fix is prioritisation, which Sameer escalates. Her next two sprints are on target.
```

Scene 9 — Delivering bad news

```text
Delivering bad news is its own skill: redundancy, a rejected promotion, a cancelled project. Say it early in the conversation, clearly, in the first minute. Don't bury it under small talk. Give the reason honestly, as far as you're able. Then stop talking, and let the person react. Acknowledge the impact. Explain what happens next and what support exists. Don't argue with emotions, and don't say, I know how you feel. For anything with HR or legal implications, follow your organisation's policy and involve HR before you start.
```

Scene 10 — Watch me: conversation plan + rehearsal

```text
Watch me prepare. I'm using the conversation plan from the lesson for an illustrative situation: a peer, Ali, keeps changing my team's priorities without telling me. Purpose: one agreed way to change priorities. Both stories: I think he's bypassing me. He may think he's being quick and helpful. Unknown: pressure from his director? Identity threat for me: I look like I can't protect my team. Opening: I'd like to talk about how priority changes reach my team. Then I ask an AI assistant to role-play Ali, a bit defensive, for six exchanges, and give me feedback. It tells me I interrupted twice.
```

Scene 11 — Did it work?

```text
How do you know it went well? Not whether it was comfortable, because it rarely is. Ask instead: did we both understand each other's view? Did we agree a specific next step, owner and date? Is the relationship intact, or even stronger? Did the behaviour or situation actually change over the following weeks? Send a short follow-up note the same day summarising what you agreed. And book a check-in. Many difficult conversations need a second, shorter conversation once both people have had time to think.
```

Scene 12 — Common mistakes

```text
Common mistakes. Waiting until you're angry. Rehearsing only your argument, not their story. Opening with a verdict: we need to talk about your attitude. Sending a long message instead of meeting. Burying bad news. Arguing with feelings. Trying to win the what happened conversation and losing the relationship. And using an AI role-play as a prediction of what the other person will say. It's rehearsal, useful for your wording and nerves, not a crystal ball.
```

Scene 13 — Recap and try this now

```text
Let's recap. Every difficult conversation has three layers: what happened, feelings and identity. Prepare using a clear purpose, both stories, named feelings and a neutral opening. In the room: open briefly, listen to learn, share your view as your view and solve together. Deliver bad news early and honestly. Your try this now: take the conversation you've been avoiding, complete the plan in the lesson in twenty minutes, and book the conversation within a week. Next, we'll look at conflict within teams and how to mediate it.
```

</details>

<details><summary>Narration per scene (paste into ElevenLabs)</summary>

Scene 1 — Resolving conflict

```text
Two of your best people have stopped talking to each other. Meetings are tense. Messages are cc'd to you as evidence. Everyone else is walking on eggshells. Sound familiar? Conflict is part of any team that cares about its work. The question isn't how to avoid it, but how to make it productive. In this lecture, you'll learn the five conflict styles and when each fits, how to move from positions to interests, a simple mediation process for leaders, and when to stop and escalate. By the end, you'll have a mediation kit ready to use.
```

Scene 2 — Why it matters

```text
Why does this matter? Unresolved conflict is expensive. It slows decisions, drains energy and, left alone, pushes good people out. But the absence of conflict isn't a good sign either. Teams that never disagree are often suppressing ideas, and they make worse decisions. What you want is healthy conflict about tasks and ideas, and very little conflict about personalities. Your role as a leader is to keep disagreement focused on the work, surface it early, and help people resolve it themselves wherever possible.
```

Scene 3 — Five conflict styles

```text
First, the concept of conflict styles. A widely used framework, the Thomas-Kilmann model, describes five styles based on two dimensions: how assertive you are about your own concerns, and how cooperative you are with the other person's. Competing: high assertive, low cooperative. Accommodating: the reverse. Avoiding: low on both. Compromising: middle on both. And collaborating: high on both. Here's the key idea: each style has a place. Competing fits a genuine emergency. Avoiding fits a trivial issue. But for important issues with ongoing relationships, collaborating is usually worth the extra time.
```

Scene 4 — Styles are gears

```text
Here's an analogy. Conflict styles are like the gears in a car. First gear is powerful but noisy, fine for pulling away, terrible on the motorway. Top gear is efficient at speed, useless on a steep hill. A good driver changes gear for the road. Most of us, though, have a favourite gear we use everywhere. Some always compete. Some always avoid. Notice your default gear. Then ask, before each conflict, what does this road actually need?
```

Scene 5 — Positions and interests

```text
Next, positions and interests, which you may know from negotiation. A position is what someone says they want. I need the designer full-time on my project. An interest is why. My launch date is fixed and I'm worried about quality. When two people argue positions, only one can win. When you uncover interests, you often find solutions that satisfy both. So ask: what's important to you about that? What are you worried might happen? Then look for options that meet both sets of interests.
```

Scene 6 — A mediation process

```text
Now a mediation process you can run as a team lead. Step one: meet each person separately first. Understand their view, their interests and what they'd need. Step two: bring them together with simple ground rules: one speaker at a time, describe behaviour not character. Step three: each person explains their view without interruption, and the other paraphrases it back. Step four: you summarise shared interests and differences. Step five: generate options together, at least five before judging. Step six: agree specifics and put them in writing. Then check in two weeks later.
```

Scene 7 — Worked example 1: Ella and Josh (illustrative)

```text
A simple worked example. In a small agency in Birmingham, the copywriter, Ella, and the designer, Josh, keep clashing over deadlines. Ella says Josh never sends layouts on time. Josh says Ella changes the copy after he's designed around it. Their manager, Priya, meets each of them first, then together. Interests surface: Ella wants time to refine, Josh wants stable copy. They agree a simple rule: copy is locked before design starts, with one agreed revision window. The clashes stop within a fortnight.
```

Scene 8 — Worked example 2: Jeddah distributor (illustrative)

```text
Now a realistic business scenario, illustrative. At a hospital supplies distributor in Jeddah, the sales manager, Abdullah, and the warehouse manager, Noura, are in open conflict. Sales promises next-day delivery to win orders; the warehouse misses them and gets blamed. The regional director mediates. In separate meetings she hears the interests: sales needs a competitive promise, the warehouse needs predictable volume. Together they agree options: a same-day cut-off time, a daily volume forecast from sales, and a shared on-time delivery measure owned by both. Within two months, missed deliveries fall and the blame emails stop.
```

Scene 9 — When to escalate

```text
Now, a crucial boundary. Not every conflict should be mediated by a line manager. If there are allegations of harassment, bullying, discrimination, threats, safety risks or potential misconduct, stop. Those are not disagreements to smooth over. Follow your organisation's policy, document what you've been told, and involve HR or the appropriate function straight away. Mediating them informally can harm the person who raised the concern and expose the organisation. It's also why, in the separate pre-meetings, you ask: is there anything that would make a joint conversation unsafe for you?
```

Scene 10 — Watch me: the mediation kit

```text
Watch me do it. An illustrative scenario: two team leads, Adeel and Fatima, disagree about who owns client onboarding. I open the mediation kit. In the separate meetings, I ask the five questions and note interests. Adeel wants clear accountability. Fatima wants to protect her team's time. In the joint meeting, I set the ground rules and ask each to paraphrase the other. Then options: a shared checklist, a single owner per client, a handover meeting. We choose single owner by client size, and I type the working agreement table into our shared doc while they watch.
```

Scene 11 — Resolved or just quiet?

```text
How do you know the conflict is resolved, not just quiet? Look at behaviour, not words. Are they communicating directly rather than through you? Are hand-offs working? Have the cc'd evidence emails stopped? Do they both describe the agreement the same way when you ask separately? At the two-week check-in, ask each person, what's working, and what still needs adjusting? And look at yourself. If you're still the go-between, you haven't resolved the conflict. You've just absorbed it.
```

Scene 12 — Common mistakes

```text
Common mistakes. Ignoring conflict and hoping it'll fade. Taking sides based on who complains first or loudest. Holding the joint meeting without meeting separately first. Focusing on personalities instead of behaviours and interests. Imposing a solution nobody owns. Mediating something that's actually a policy matter. And forgetting the follow-up. Most agreements need at least one adjustment, and without a check-in, small cracks reopen.
```

Scene 13 — Recap and try this now

```text
Let's recap. Conflict is normal, and task conflict can be healthy. Know the five styles and your own default, and choose the right gear for the road. Move people from positions to interests. Use the six-step mediation process, and escalate anything involving harassment, safety or misconduct. Your try this now: think of one tension in your team, run the separate pre-meeting questions from the lesson with each person this week, and decide whether a joint meeting is the right next step. Next module, we'll turn to meetings, writing and decisions.
```

</details>

### 7. Meetings that work

In this lecture, you'll learn a four-step method: decide whether it should be a meeting at all, design it, run it well, and close it properly. We'll also cover hybrid meetings and AI notetakers. | Meetings that work<br>• Should it be a meeting?<br>• Design<br>• Run<br>• Close and follow up<br>• Hybrid and AI notetakers | Layout: a crowded weekly calendar grid. 0:00 meeting blocks pile in until the week is solid; 0:15 a timer counts total hours; 0:25 four step chips appear: Question, Design, Run, Close; 0:40 two extra chips for hybrid and AI. |
| 2 | 0:41 | 37 | Why does this matter? Because a one-hour meeting with eight people isn't an hour. It's eight hours of your team's time. Good meetings do the opposite. They produce decisions and commitments that would take days of messages to reach. The aim isn't fewer meetings for the sake of it. It's that every meeting earns its place. | Why it matters<br>• 1 hour × 8 people = 8 hours<br>• Delayed and revisited decisions<br>• Quiet voices lost<br>• Every meeting must earn its place | Layout: a clock with eight small avatar icons around it. 0:00 the minute hand sweeps once; 0:10 a counter multiplies '1 hour' into '8 hours'; 0:25 a looping arrow labelled 'revisited again'; 0:40 a badge 'earns its place' stamps onto a single meeting block. |
| 3 | 1:18 | 42 | Here's the concept. Every meeting should have one main purpose. Decide. Generate ideas. Align, meaning get people on the same page and surface concerns. <details><summary>Narration per scene (paste into ElevenLabs)</summary>

Scene 1 — Meetings that work

```text
Think about the last meeting you left wondering why you were there. No clear purpose, the same three people talking, and at the end, nobody quite sure what was decided. Now multiply that by every recurring meeting in your week. Meetings are one of the biggest uses of a team's time, and one of the easiest places to win it back. In this lecture, you'll learn a four-step method: decide whether it should be a meeting at all, design it, run it well, and close it properly. We'll also cover hybrid meetings and AI notetakers.
```

Scene 2 — Why it matters

```text
Why does this matter? Because a one-hour meeting with eight people isn't an hour. It's eight hours of your team's time. Bad meetings also have hidden costs: decisions get delayed, the same topic is revisited again and again, and the quieter people, often with the best ideas, stop contributing. Good meetings do the opposite. They produce decisions and commitments that would take days of messages to reach. The aim isn't fewer meetings for the sake of it. It's that every meeting earns its place.
```

Scene 3 — One purpose per meeting

```text
Here's the concept. Every meeting should have one main purpose. Decide. Generate ideas. Align, meaning get people on the same page and surface concerns. Or inform, and inform is the one that should most often be an email, a document or a short recorded video instead. Once you know the purpose, write the outcome in a single sentence: by the end, we will have chosen a supplier, or by the end, we will have three options for the Eid campaign. Here's the key idea. If you can't write that sentence, you're not ready to send the invite.
```

Scene 4 — The flight

```text
Here's an analogy. A good meeting is like a well-run flight. Before take-off, there's a flight plan: destination, route, fuel. During the flight, the pilot keeps to the route, handles turbulence, and doesn't take detours because someone fancies a look at the mountains. At landing, there's a clear arrival and everyone knows where to collect their bags. Many meetings take off without a destination, circle for an hour, and land somewhere nobody expected. Your job is to be the pilot: plan, fly the route, and land deliberately.
```

Scene 5 — Design

```text
Step two, design. Build an agenda with owners and timings, and for each item say whether it's a decision or a discussion. Invite only the people you need, and be clear about each person's role: decider, input, or informed afterwards. Send a short pre-read at least a day before, with two or three questions to consider. Then use the first few minutes of the meeting for silent reading if people haven't read it. Some companies famously start meetings with a silent read of a written memo. It levels the playing field and speeds up everything that follows.
```

Scene 6 — Run and close

```text
Step three, run it. Start on time, restate the purpose and outcome, and name the decision rule. Keep to the agenda and park off-topic items visibly in a parking lot. Draw in quieter voices deliberately, for example with a quick round or by asking people to write ideas first. In hybrid meetings, fairness takes extra effort. If one person is remote, everyone joins from their own screen, and the chair checks the chat and the remote faces first. Step four, close. Summarise decisions, owners, deadlines and what you'll tell others. Then send it in writing within a day.
```

Scene 7 — Worked example 1: Tariq in Lahore (illustrative)

```text
A simple worked example. Tariq runs a small property agency in Lahore. His Monday team meeting has drifted to ninety minutes of updates. He changes it. Updates go into a shared document by Sunday evening. The meeting shrinks to thirty minutes with only two items: blockers, and one decision for the week. On the first week, the decision is whether to pause a slow-moving listing campaign. They decide in ten minutes, because everyone read the numbers beforehand. The team gets an hour back every week.
```

Scene 8 — Worked example 2: Hannah in Austin (illustrative)

```text
Now a realistic business scenario, illustrative. Hannah is chief of staff at a fifty-person software firm in Austin. The leadership team meets for two hours every Tuesday, yet decisions keep slipping. She runs a calendar audit and finds that most agenda items are updates. She redesigns the meeting: a written pre-read due Monday, silent reading for the first ten minutes, and only items marked decide or align on the agenda. Each decision gets an owner and a date in the close. Within a quarter, the meeting is down to seventy-five minutes and the backlog of open decisions has roughly halved.
```

Scene 9 — AI notetakers

```text
Now AI notetakers. Many video platforms can transcribe and summarise meetings, depending on your plan and settings. They're genuinely useful for people who missed the call, and for catching action items. But set rules. Announce it at the start and respect anyone who asks to switch it off. Don't use it in one-to-ones, HR, health or disciplinary conversations. Have a named person check the summary before it's shared, because AI can misattribute who said what or invent a decision that wasn't made. And remember recording is subject to privacy law and workplace policy, so check with your data protection lead.
```

Scene 10 — Watch me: meeting design card

```text
Watch me do it. I'm designing a one-hour meeting for an illustrative product launch decision. Purpose: decide. Outcome: by the end, we will have chosen launch date A or B. Pre-read: a two-page comparison, sent tomorrow morning, with three questions. Agenda: ten minutes silent read, twenty minutes risks for each option, fifteen minutes decision, fifteen minutes actions and comms. Attendees: two deciders, three inputs. I cut two people who only need to be informed and add them to the summary list. Decision rule: the product director decides after hearing input. I paste the card into the invite.
```

Scene 11 — Measuring success

```text
How do you measure whether meetings are improving? Track a few simple signals. Hours per person per week in meetings. The percentage of meetings with a written outcome. How many decisions carry an owner and a date. And ask attendees a one-question pulse at the end of the month: which meeting would you cancel, and why? You'll be surprised how honest people are. Then act on it. A calendar audit every quarter keeps meeting creep in check.
```

Scene 12 — Common mistakes

```text
Common mistakes. Meetings with no stated purpose. Information-only meetings that should be emails. Inviting everyone just in case. No pre-read, or a pre-read nobody reads. Letting the loudest voice dominate. Ending without owners and dates. Hybrid meetings where remote people are an afterthought. And treating an unchecked AI summary as the official record of what was decided.
```

Scene 13 — Recap and try this now

```text
Let's recap. Every meeting needs one purpose and a written outcome. Design it with an agenda, the right people and a pre-read. Run it on time, with fair participation and a parking lot. Close with decisions, owners, dates and communication. Set clear rules for AI notetakers. Your try this now: do the fifteen-minute calendar audit in the lesson, and cancel, shorten or convert at least one recurring meeting to an async update this month. Next, we'll look at writing: how to write messages that busy people actually read.
```

</details>
