The pyramid principle, developed by Barbara Minto, formalises this: start with the main point, group supporting arguments beneath it, and support each with evidence. Here's the key idea. Write in the order the reader needs, not the order you discovered things. Your discovery journey is interesting to you. Your reader wants the destination. | BLUF and the pyramid<br>• Bottom line first<br>• Then reasons<br>• Then detail<br>• Minto's pyramid principle<br>• Reader's order, not discovery order | Layout: a pyramid. 0:00 the top block 'Main point' drops in; 0:10 three blocks beneath 'Reason 1, 2, 3'; 0:20 a base row of evidence blocks; 0:35 a winding path labelled 'your discovery journey' fades, replaced by a straight arrow to the top. |
| 4 | 1:47 | 36 | Here's an analogy. Think of a newspaper story. The headline tells you what happened. The first paragraph tells you who, what, where and when. Each later paragraph adds detail you can skip if you're busy. Editors call it the inverted pyramid, and it's designed for readers who might stop at any moment. Your emails should work the same way. If your reader stops after the subject line, do they know what you need? After the first sentence, do they know the answer? | The newspaper test<br>• Headline = subject line<br>• First paragraph = bottom line<br>• Later paragraphs = skippable detail<br>• Could they stop at any point? | Layout: a newspaper front page. 0:00 the headline highlights; 0:10 the first paragraph highlights; 0:20 later paragraphs dim progressively; 0:30 an email appears beside it with matching highlights on subject line and first sentence. |
| 5 | 2:23 | 40 | Now some techniques for clarity. Use a subject line that says what kind of message this is and when a response is needed, like decision needed, CRM renewal, by Thursday. Keep sentences short, and use one idea per paragraph. Prefer plain words: use rather than utilise, help rather than facilitate. Use the active voice: the team missed the deadline, rather than the deadline was missed. Use bullets for lists, and bold sparingly for the one thing people mustn't miss. And make the ask specific: what you need, from whom, by when. | Clarity techniques<br>• Informative subject lines<br>• Short sentences, one idea per paragraph<br>• Plain words, active voice<br>• Bullets for lists<br>• Specific asks: what, who, when | Layout: a 'before' sentence on screen: 'Facilitation of the utilisation of the platform was undertaken.' 0:10 words strike through and rewrite to 'We helped the team use the platform.'; 0:25 a subject line builds with three tags; 0:40 an ask card with three fields. |
| 6 | 3:03 | 42 | A simple worked example. Mehwish, a project coordinator in Karachi, needs her manager to approve an extra freelancer. Her first draft begins: Hi, hope you're well. As you know, we've been having a busy few weeks, and there have been a few challenges. The ask is at the bottom. She rewrites it. Subject: approval needed, one extra freelancer, by Wednesday. First line: I'd like approval to hire one freelancer for three weeks at the agreed day rate to keep the client launch on time. Then three bullets of reasons. Her manager approves it in four minutes. | Worked example 1: Mehwish (illustrative)<br>• Draft: ask at the bottom<br>• Rewrite: subject says 'approval needed'<br>• First line = the request<br>• 3 bullets of reasons<br>• Approved in 4 minutes | Layout: two email drafts side by side. 0:00 the left draft with the ask highlighted at the bottom; 0:15 the right draft builds with a clear subject and first line; 0:35 a reply notification 'Approved' appears with a timer showing four minutes. |
| 7 | 3:45 | 40 | Now a realistic business scenario, illustrative. At a consumer goods company in Dubai, the regional marketing lead, Rashid, sends a monthly performance report to the country managers. It's twelve pages. Almost nobody replies, and the same questions keep coming up in meetings. He restructures it. Page one is a BLUF summary: three things that changed, one decision needed from each country, and a traffic-light table. The detail moves to an appendix. Replies go from almost none to most of the country managers within a week, and the monthly meeting drops by thirty minutes. | Worked example 2: Rashid in Dubai (illustrative)<br>• 12-page report, few replies<br>• Page one: 3 changes, 1 decision each, traffic lights<br>• Detail to appendix<br>• Replies up; meeting 30 minutes shorter | Layout: a twelve-page document stack. 0:00 the stack sits untouched with a 'no replies' counter; 0:15 a single summary page lifts off the top with three change bullets and a traffic-light table; 0:35 remaining pages slide into an 'Appendix' folder; 0:50 a reply counter climbs. |
| 8 | 4:25 | 38 | Tone and culture matter too. In some workplaces, especially in parts of South Asia and the Gulf, a warmer opening and more courtesy are expected, and a message that starts with the ask can feel abrupt. In many UK and US workplaces, brevity is read as respect. You don't have to choose between clarity and courtesy. A one-line greeting followed by the bottom line works almost everywhere. Also consider readers working in a second language: shorter sentences, fewer idioms, and no sarcasm, which rarely survives in writing. | Tone and culture<br>• Warmth expected in some cultures<br>• Brevity = respect in others<br>• One-line greeting + bottom line<br>• Second-language readers: short, literal, no sarcasm | Layout: an email template with an adjustable 'warmth' slider. 0:00 the slider moves and the greeting line lengthens or shortens while the bottom line stays fixed; 0:30 an idiom 'ballpark figure' crosses out and becomes 'rough estimate'. |
| 9 | 5:03 | 44 | Different formats need different shapes. In chat, lead with the point in one message rather than five fragments, and never send just hi, then wait for a reply. Say what you need in the first message. In email, the subject line does half the work. In a document or proposal, add a short executive summary at the top, headings that say something, like costs rise in quarter three, rather than costs, and an appendix for the detail. And for anything sensitive or emotional, writing is usually the wrong format altogether. Pick up the phone or book a call, then confirm in writing. | Different formats<br>• Chat: the point in one message, not 'hi'<br>• Email: the subject does half the work<br>• Docs: summary + headings that say something<br>• Sensitive? | 10 | 5:47 | 47 | Watch me do it. I take an illustrative draft about a CRM renewal where the ask sits in paragraph four. I paste the draft into an AI assistant with the editing prompt from the lesson: bottom line first, cut by thirty per cent, plain British English, no new facts. It shortens it well, but it adds a claim about pricing I never made. I delete that line. Then I read it aloud and put back one phrase that sounds like me. | Watch me: BLUF rewrite + AI edit<br>• Bottom line first<br>• 3 reasons, 1 ask with a deadline<br>• AI trims 30%<br>• Remove an invented claim<br>• Read aloud; restore your voice | Layout: screen-recording style editor. 0:00 a long draft; 0:15 a bottom-line sentence moves to the top; 0:30 a side chat returns a shorter version; 0:40 one sentence highlights red 'not in original' and is deleted; 0:55 a speaker icon plays as the text is read aloud. |

<details><summary>Narration per scene (paste into ElevenLabs)</summary>

Scene 1 — Clear writing

```text
Here's an uncomfortable truth. Most of the people who receive your emails, chat messages and documents won't read them fully. They'll skim the first line or two, decide whether it matters, and move on. If your key point is in paragraph four, it may as well not exist. In this lecture you'll learn how to write for skimmers: bottom line up front, the pyramid principle, practical techniques for clarity, how tone shifts across cultures, and how to use AI assistants to edit without losing your voice. By the end, you'll rewrite one real message.
```

Scene 2 — Why it matters

```text
Why does this matter? Because most work now happens in writing. Email, chat, tickets, documents and proposals. Remote and hybrid teams depend on it even more. Unclear writing costs time: follow-up questions, misunderstandings, and decisions made on the wrong assumption. Clear writing is also a signal of clear thinking. Senior people notice who can explain a complex issue in a few lines. That's often who gets trusted with the next big piece of work.
```

Scene 3 — BLUF and the pyramid

```text
Here's the concept. BLUF, bottom line up front, comes from military writing. Put the conclusion, recommendation or request first, then the supporting reasons, then the detail. The pyramid principle, developed by Barbara Minto, formalises this: start with the main point, group supporting arguments beneath it, and support each with evidence. Here's the key idea. Write in the order the reader needs, not the order you discovered things. Your discovery journey is interesting to you. Your reader wants the destination.
```

Scene 4 — The newspaper test

```text
Here's an analogy. Think of a newspaper story. The headline tells you what happened. The first paragraph tells you who, what, where and when. Each later paragraph adds detail you can skip if you're busy. Editors call it the inverted pyramid, and it's designed for readers who might stop at any moment. Your emails should work the same way. If your reader stops after the subject line, do they know what you need? After the first sentence, do they know the answer?
```

Scene 5 — Clarity techniques

```text
Now some techniques for clarity. Use a subject line that says what kind of message this is and when a response is needed, like decision needed, CRM renewal, by Thursday. Keep sentences short, and use one idea per paragraph. Prefer plain words: use rather than utilise, help rather than facilitate. Use the active voice: the team missed the deadline, rather than the deadline was missed. Use bullets for lists, and bold sparingly for the one thing people mustn't miss. And make the ask specific: what you need, from whom, by when.
```

Scene 6 — Worked example 1: Mehwish (illustrative)

```text
A simple worked example. Mehwish, a project coordinator in Karachi, needs her manager to approve an extra freelancer. Her first draft begins: Hi, hope you're well. As you know, we've been having a busy few weeks, and there have been a few challenges. The ask is at the bottom. She rewrites it. Subject: approval needed, one extra freelancer, by Wednesday. First line: I'd like approval to hire one freelancer for three weeks at the agreed day rate to keep the client launch on time. Then three bullets of reasons. Her manager approves it in four minutes.
```

Scene 7 — Worked example 2: Rashid in Dubai (illustrative)

```text
Now a realistic business scenario, illustrative. At a consumer goods company in Dubai, the regional marketing lead, Rashid, sends a monthly performance report to the country managers. It's twelve pages. Almost nobody replies, and the same questions keep coming up in meetings. He restructures it. Page one is a BLUF summary: three things that changed, one decision needed from each country, and a traffic-light table. The detail moves to an appendix. Replies go from almost none to most of the country managers within a week, and the monthly meeting drops by thirty minutes.
```

Scene 8 — Tone and culture

```text
Tone and culture matter too. In some workplaces, especially in parts of South Asia and the Gulf, a warmer opening and more courtesy are expected, and a message that starts with the ask can feel abrupt. In many UK and US workplaces, brevity is read as respect. You don't have to choose between clarity and courtesy. A one-line greeting followed by the bottom line works almost everywhere. Also consider readers working in a second language: shorter sentences, fewer idioms, and no sarcasm, which rarely survives in writing.
```

Scene 9 — Different formats

```text
Different formats need different shapes. In chat, lead with the point in one message rather than five fragments, and never send just hi, then wait for a reply. Say what you need in the first message. In email, the subject line does half the work. In a document or proposal, add a short executive summary at the top, headings that say something, like costs rise in quarter three, rather than costs, and an appendix for the detail. And for anything sensitive or emotional, writing is usually the wrong format altogether. Pick up the phone or book a call, then confirm in writing.
```

Scene 10 — Watch me: BLUF rewrite + AI edit

```text
Watch me do it. I take an illustrative draft about a CRM renewal where the ask sits in paragraph four. First, I write the bottom line: I recommend renewing for twelve months, not thirty-six. Then three reasons. Then the ask: approve or reject by Thursday at five. I paste the draft into an AI assistant with the editing prompt from the lesson: bottom line first, cut by thirty per cent, plain British English, no new facts. It shortens it well, but it adds a claim about pricing I never made. I delete that line. Then I read it aloud and put back one phrase that sounds like me.
```

Scene 11 — Measuring success

```text
How do you measure clear writing? Look at the responses. Fewer follow-up questions. Faster replies to decision requests. Decisions made on the first round, not the third. You can also test yourself before sending: could someone who reads only the subject and first sentence act correctly? And every so often, ask a trusted colleague to read one of your messages and tell you, in one sentence, what you're asking for. If their sentence doesn't match yours, rewrite.
```

Scene 12 — Common mistakes

```text
Common mistakes. Burying the ask. Vague subject lines like quick question or update. Long paragraphs covering five topics. Jargon and acronyms the reader doesn't know. Passive voice that hides who's responsible. Writing in anger and sending immediately. Copying the whole company when three people need it. And pasting AI-generated text without checking it, which can add facts you never said and a voice that sounds like nobody.
```

Scene 13 — Recap and try this now

```text
Let's recap. Readers skim, so put the bottom line up front. Structure with the pyramid: main point, reasons, evidence. Use clear subject lines, short sentences, plain words, active voice and a specific ask. Adapt warmth to culture but keep the bottom line clear. Use AI to edit, then check facts and put your voice back. Your try this now: take one message you need to send today, rewrite it with the BLUF template in the lesson, and notice how quickly you get a response. Next, we'll look at how groups make better decisions.
```

</details>

<details><summary>Narration per scene (paste into ElevenLabs)</summary>

Scene 1 — Group decisions

```text
Have you ever sat in a meeting where everyone nodded along to a decision, and then, in the corridor afterwards, half of them said they thought it was a mistake? That's not a people problem. It's a process problem. Groups can make much better decisions than individuals, because they bring more information and perspectives. But only if you design the process. In this lecture, you'll learn why group decisions go wrong, how to clarify who decides and how, how to frame and discuss a decision well, and how to decide and commit, including using a pre-mortem.
```

Scene 2 — Why it matters

```text
Why does it matter? Because the quality of a team's decisions compounds. A slightly better choice on priorities, hiring, pricing or suppliers, made again and again, adds up to a very different business over a year. And groups have well-known traps. Groupthink, where the desire for harmony suppresses disagreement. Anchoring on the first idea mentioned. The most senior or loudest person steering the room. And endless debate with no clear decider. Each of these has a practical fix.
```

Scene 3 — Clarify the process first

```text
Here's the first concept. Before discussing the content, clarify the process. Who decides? One person, a small group, or everyone? And what's the rule? Consult, where the decider hears input and then decides. Consent, where the proposal goes ahead unless someone has a reasoned objection. Vote. Or full consensus, which is slow and best kept for decisions everyone must live with deeply. Frameworks such as RAPID, from Bain, or DACI, used in many product teams, name the roles explicitly. Here's the key idea: most decision conflict is really confusion about who decides.
```

Scene 4 — Jury, referee or family holiday?

```text
Here's an analogy. Think of a jury, a referee and a family choosing a holiday. A jury must reach a verdict by a defined rule. A referee decides alone, instantly, after taking in what they can see. A family discusses, but in the end one or two people book the flights. Each is a legitimate way to decide. Chaos comes when people think they're on a jury, but actually there's a referee. Tell people which game they're playing before the discussion starts.
```

Scene 5 — Frame and discuss

```text
Next, frame the decision well. Instead of a yes or no question, like should we launch in Saudi Arabia, frame it with options: which market should we enter next, Saudi Arabia, Qatar, or deepen the UAE? That invites real comparison. Then improve the discussion. Ask people to write their views independently before anyone speaks, so the first opinion doesn't anchor everyone. Have the most senior person speak last. Assign someone to argue the other side. And separate facts from assumptions on a whiteboard as you go.
```

Scene 6 — Decide and commit

```text
Then decide and commit. Distinguish between one-way doors and two-way doors, a phrase popularised by Amazon's shareholder letters. A two-way door is a decision that's easy to reverse: decide fast, learn, adjust. A one-way door is hard to reverse: slow down and do more work. Once the decision is made, ask for disagree and commit. People can disagree openly during the discussion, but once it's decided, everyone supports it fully. Record dissent, and set a review date or a signal that would make you revisit.
```

Scene 7 — Worked example 1: Grace's agency (illustrative)

```text
A simple worked example. A four-person social media agency in Manchester must choose a new project management tool. Last time, the debate dragged on for a month. This time the founder, Grace, states the process: she'll decide, after input, by Friday. It's a two-way door, since they can switch in a few months. Everyone writes their top criteria independently. They compare two options against those criteria in thirty minutes. Grace decides on Friday. One designer disagrees, says so, and commits. They review in three months.
```

Scene 8 — Worked example 2: Karachi retail board (illustrative)

```text
Now a realistic business scenario, illustrative. The board of a family-owned retail chain in Karachi must decide whether to open a large new store in a new mall. It's a one-way door: a long lease and significant capital. The chief executive frames three options: open the large store, open a smaller format, or invest the same capital in e-commerce. Before discussion, each director writes their view. The finance director argues against the preferred option. Then they run a pre-mortem, which surfaces a risk nobody had raised: parking and access during mall construction. They choose the smaller format, with a clause to expand.
```

Scene 9 — The pre-mortem (Gary Klein)

```text
Let's zoom in on the pre-mortem, a technique developed by the psychologist Gary Klein. Before committing, say: imagine it's a year from now, and this decision has failed badly. Why did it fail? Everyone writes reasons silently, then shares them in turn. It works because it gives people permission to voice doubts without seeming negative. The group is no longer criticising the plan; it's explaining a hypothetical failure. You then pick the top three risks and agree a mitigation or an early warning signal for each. Twenty minutes can save a year.
```

Scene 10 — Watch me: decision record

```text
Watch me do it. I'm filling in a decision record for an illustrative choice: whether our team adopts an AI assistant for first drafts of client reports. Question with options: adopt tool A, adopt tool B, or wait six months. Decider: head of client services, after consult. Type: two-way door, but with a one-way element, data handling, so our data protection lead is an input. I ask an AI assistant to argue the strongest case against our preferred option. It raises client confidentiality and review time. I add both to the risks, and we set a review date after a sixty-day pilot.
```

Scene 11 — Measuring decision quality

```text
How do you measure decision quality? Not only by outcomes, because good decisions can have bad luck. Also judge the process. Was the decider clear? Were options compared? Were assumptions tested? Was dissent heard? Keep decision records, and at the review date, compare what you expected with what happened. Over time, you'll see patterns, such as always underestimating time, or overestimating demand. That's how teams learn to decide better.
```

Scene 12 — Common mistakes

```text
Common mistakes. Starting the debate before agreeing who decides. Yes or no framing. Letting the boss speak first. Mistaking silence for agreement. Treating every decision as a one-way door, so everything is slow, or every decision as a two-way door, so big risks are rushed. Re-opening decisions endlessly. And using an AI's answer as if it were evidence. It's a useful sparring partner, but it doesn't know your context.
```

Scene 13 — Recap and try this now

```text
Let's recap. Clarify who decides and by which rule before discussing content. Frame decisions with options. Collect views independently, let senior people speak last, and name a devil's advocate. Match your speed to one-way or two-way doors, run a pre-mortem on big calls, then disagree and commit. Record decisions and review them. Your try this now: pick one decision your team faces this month and complete the decision record in the lesson before the meeting. Next module, we move on to stakeholders and influence.
```

</details>

In this lecture, you'll learn how to identify stakeholders, map them by power and interest, engage each group appropriately, and review your map as things change. Stakeholder management isn't politics in the negative sense. It's respecting that other people have legitimate interests and giving them the right information at the right time. | Why it matters<br>• They control budget, approvals, people, adoption<br>• Consulted → help<br>• Surprised → resist<br>• Legitimate interests, right information, right time | Layout: a central project node with five resource pipes labelled budget, approvals, people, data, adoption. 0:00 pipes flow; 0:15 a 'surprised' face icon closes one valve; 0:30 a 'consulted' face icon opens it wider. |
| 3 | 1:13 | 40 | Here's the concept. Step one, identify stakeholders widely. Think about who's affected, who decides, who influences the deciders, who has the expertise, and who might block. Interest on the other, meaning how much they care. This is often called the power-interest grid, associated with Mendelow. Here's the key idea: different quadrants need different engagement, and treating everyone the same wastes time and annoys people. | Identify and map<br>• Affected, decide, influence, expertise, block<br>• Include externals<br>• Power-interest grid (Mendelow)<br>• Different quadrants, different engagement | Layout: a brainstorm cloud of role icons. 0:00 icons pop in; 0:15 they fly onto a 2x2 grid with axes 'power' and 'interest'; 0:35 quadrants label themselves: manage closely, keep satisfied, keep informed, monitor. |
| 4 | 1:53 | 37 | Here's an analogy. Think of planning a wedding. The couple's parents are high power and high interest: you consult them closely. The venue manager has high power, but mainly cares about logistics: keep them satisfied with clear information. Distant cousins are very interested but have little say: keep them informed with a nice update. The neighbours: just let them know about the parking. If you consulted distant cousins on the menu and forgot to tell the venue, you'd have chaos. Projects work exactly the same way. | The wedding planner<br>• Parents: manage closely<br>• Venue: keep satisfied<br>• Cousins: keep informed<br>• Neighbours: monitor | Layout: the 2x2 grid again with illustrated wedding figures placed in each quadrant. 0:00 parents in top right; 0:10 venue manager top left; 0:20 cousins bottom right; 0:30 neighbours bottom left with a parking sign; 0:40 a chaotic version swaps them and shows confusion icons. |
| 5 | 2:30 | 42 | Step three, engage. For manage closely, involve them early in shaping the work, meet regularly and ask for their input on key decisions. For keep satisfied, give concise, relevant updates focused on what they care about, such as cost, risk or compliance, and don't overload them. For keep informed, share regular updates and invite feedback, because they're often your users and your early warning system. For monitor, a light touch. And for each key stakeholder, note what they care about, their concerns, and their current and desired stance. The gap between those is your engagement plan. | Engage<br>• Manage closely: shape together<br>• Keep satisfied: concise, their priorities<br>• Keep informed: updates + feedback<br>• Monitor: light touch<br>• Current vs desired stance = plan | Layout: four quadrant cards expand one at a time, each revealing its engagement method with a small icon: handshake, one-page note, newsletter, eye. 0:40 a stance bar for one stakeholder shows 'neutral' moving to 'supportive' with an arrow labelled 'plan'. |
| 6 | 3:12 | 42 | A simple worked example. Adnan, an IT lead at a college in Lahore, wants to move all staff to a new learning platform. He maps stakeholders. The principal: high power, medium interest, keep satisfied with a cost and risk summary. Heads of department: high power, high interest, manage closely through a design workshop. <details><summary>Narration per scene (paste into ElevenLabs)</summary>

Scene 1 — Stakeholder management

```text
Here's a pattern you may recognise. A project is going well. The team is delivering. And then, three weeks before launch, someone you barely thought about, a finance director, a regional head, a legal adviser, raises an objection that stops everything. Most projects don't fail for technical reasons. They stall because the right people weren't involved at the right time. In this lecture, you'll learn how to identify stakeholders, map them by power and interest, engage each group appropriately, and review your map as things change. By the end, you'll have a working stakeholder map.
```

Scene 2 — Why it matters

```text
Why does this matter? Because stakeholders control the things your work depends on: budget, approvals, people, data and adoption. A stakeholder who feels consulted is far more likely to help you. One who feels surprised is far more likely to resist, even if your idea is good. Stakeholder management isn't politics in the negative sense. It's respecting that other people have legitimate interests and giving them the right information at the right time.
```

Scene 3 — Identify and map

```text
Here's the concept. Step one, identify stakeholders widely. Think about who's affected, who decides, who influences the deciders, who has the expertise, and who might block. Include people outside the organisation too: clients, suppliers, regulators. Step two, map them on a simple grid. Power on one axis, meaning their ability to affect the outcome. Interest on the other, meaning how much they care. This is often called the power-interest grid, associated with Mendelow. Here's the key idea: different quadrants need different engagement, and treating everyone the same wastes time and annoys people.
```

Scene 4 — The wedding planner

```text
Here's an analogy. Think of planning a wedding. The couple's parents are high power and high interest: you consult them closely. The venue manager has high power, but mainly cares about logistics: keep them satisfied with clear information. Distant cousins are very interested but have little say: keep them informed with a nice update. The neighbours: just let them know about the parking. If you consulted distant cousins on the menu and forgot to tell the venue, you'd have chaos. Projects work exactly the same way.
```

Scene 5 — Engage

```text
Step three, engage. For manage closely, involve them early in shaping the work, meet regularly and ask for their input on key decisions. For keep satisfied, give concise, relevant updates focused on what they care about, such as cost, risk or compliance, and don't overload them. For keep informed, share regular updates and invite feedback, because they're often your users and your early warning system. For monitor, a light touch. And for each key stakeholder, note what they care about, their concerns, and their current and desired stance. The gap between those is your engagement plan.
```

Scene 6 — Worked example 1: Adnan's college (illustrative)

```text
A simple worked example. Adnan, an IT lead at a college in Lahore, wants to move all staff to a new learning platform. He maps stakeholders. The principal: high power, medium interest, keep satisfied with a cost and risk summary. Heads of department: high power, high interest, manage closely through a design workshop. Teachers: high interest, lower power, keep informed and invite them to a pilot. Students: keep informed. The IT support contractor: keep satisfied. Because heads of department helped design the rollout, they champion it, and adoption is much smoother than the last system change.
```

Scene 7 — Worked example 2: Layla in Abu Dhabi (illustrative)

```text
Now a realistic business scenario, illustrative. Layla is rolling out a new inventory system across twenty stores for a fashion retailer in Abu Dhabi. She maps stakeholders in a spreadsheet. The finance director cares about payback and hidden running costs: she sends a one-page monthly note with those exact numbers. Store managers are sceptical because peak season is coming: she runs a pilot in two stores and brings their feedback into the design. The legal team cares about data handling: she involves them before choosing the vendor. Launch is on time, and the store managers present the pilot results to their peers.
```

Scene 8 — Review and adapt

```text
Step four, review and adapt. Stakeholders move. A reorganisation brings in a new director. A budget cut makes finance far more interested. A project phase shifts attention from IT to operations. So review your map monthly, in fifteen minutes. Who has moved? Where's the biggest gap between current and desired stance? Who haven't you spoken to in a month? Pick three people to talk to this month. And watch for silent stakeholders: people who are affected but never speak up, often frontline staff. Silence isn't support.
```

Scene 9 — Difficult stakeholders

```text
What about a genuinely difficult stakeholder, someone powerful who seems to oppose everything? Start by assuming they have a reason. Meet them one to one, early, and ask: what would need to be true for you to support this? What worries you most? Often the objection is about a risk they own, like compliance, budget or their team's workload, and you can design around it. Involve them in solving that part. If they still disagree, make sure their concern is recorded and escalated fairly to the decider, not ignored. People can accept losing an argument. They rarely accept being bypassed.
```

Scene 10 — Watch me: spreadsheet stakeholder map

```text
Watch me do it. I open a spreadsheet for an illustrative project, launching a client self-service portal. I list twelve stakeholders by role and score power and interest from one to five. I add the quadrant formula from the lesson, so the quadrant fills itself. Then conditional formatting to highlight anyone I haven't contacted in thirty days. Two cells turn red: the head of legal and the customer service team leads. Then I ask an AI assistant, with roles only, who I might have missed. It suggests the accessibility lead and the data protection officer. Good catch. I add both.
```

Scene 11 — Measuring success

```text
How do you measure success? Look for fewer surprises late in the project. Faster approvals. Stakeholders moving from sceptical or neutral towards supportive. And people raising concerns early, directly with you, rather than in a steering committee. You can even add a simple column to your sheet for stance, and track the count of supportive key stakeholders over time. If it's not moving, change your engagement approach, not just the frequency of your emails.
```

Scene 12 — Common mistakes

```text
Common mistakes. Only thinking about senior stakeholders and forgetting users. Treating everyone the same, so senior people get overloaded and users get ignored. Engaging people only when you need something. Surprising powerful people in public meetings. Assuming silence means agreement. Letting the map go stale after week one. And pasting personal notes about named individuals into an AI tool. Keep stakeholder analysis professional and role-based, especially if it could ever be read by the people it describes.
```

Scene 13 — Recap and try this now

```text
Let's recap. Identify stakeholders widely, including those outside the organisation. Map them by power and interest. Engage each quadrant differently, and plan from the gap between current and desired stance. Review monthly, because stakeholders move. Your try this now: build the spreadsheet map from the lesson for your most important project, add the quadrant formula and the thirty-day highlight, and pick three people to talk to this month. Next, we'll look at how to influence people when you don't have authority over them.
```

</details>

<details><summary>Narration per scene (paste into ElevenLabs)</summary>

Scene 1 — Influencing without authority

```text
Imagine you need something from a team that doesn't report to you. Perhaps the data team to prioritise your dashboard, or a senior colleague to back your proposal. You can't instruct them. You can only persuade. Here's the thing: this is most of modern work. Projects cut across departments, partners and clients. The people who get things done are the ones who can influence without authority. In this lecture, you'll learn where influence comes from, how to use proven persuasion principles ethically, and a step-by-step influence plan. By the end, you'll have a plan for one real person.
```

Scene 2 — Why it matters

```text
Why does this matter? Because formal authority is shrinking as a way of getting things done. Flatter organisations, matrix structures, remote teams and cross-company projects all mean you'll need the cooperation of people you can't direct. Even people with authority get better results when they persuade rather than command. Commitment beats compliance. When people choose to help, they bring their ideas and energy. When they're forced, they do the minimum.
```

Scene 3 — Sources of influence

```text
Here's the concept. Influence comes from several sources. Expertise: people trust you know your stuff. Relationships: they know and like you. Reputation: you deliver what you promise. Information: you have useful knowledge or data. And reciprocity: you've helped them before. A helpful way to think about reciprocity comes from Allan Cohen and David Bradford's work on influence without authority. They describe currencies: things people value, such as resources, recognition, support, a sense of purpose, or simply gratitude. Here's the key idea: find out what the other person values, and offer something genuine in exchange.
```

Scene 4 — The relationship bank account

```text
Here's an analogy. Think of a bank account. Every time you help someone, share credit, give useful information or keep a promise, you make a deposit. Every time you ask for something, you make a withdrawal. If you only visit the bank when you need money, the account is empty. Influential people have made steady deposits for months before they need anything. So when they ask, the answer is usually yes. Start depositing now, with people you'll need later.
```

Scene 5 — Persuasion principles (Cialdini)

```text
Now persuasion principles. Robert Cialdini's research describes several principles that reliably influence people. Reciprocity: we want to return favours. Commitment and consistency: we like to act in line with what we've said. Social proof: we look at what peers do. Authority: we trust credible experts. Liking: we say yes to people we like. Scarcity: we value what's rare. And unity: shared identity. Used honestly, these help people make good decisions. Used dishonestly, faking scarcity or inventing social proof, they're manipulation, and they destroy trust once discovered.
```

Scene 6 — The influence plan

```text
So here's a step-by-step influence plan. First, be clear about what you need: the specific decision or action, and by when. Second, understand the other person: their goals, their pressures, what their boss measures them on this year. Third, identify what you can offer that they genuinely value. Fourth, choose the evidence they trust. Finance may want numbers, operations may want a pilot, a sales director may want customer stories. Fifth, choose the right messenger. Sometimes it's you, sometimes it's a respected peer. Sixth, prepare for objections. And seventh, ask for a small first step.
```

Scene 7 — Worked example 1: Zara in London (illustrative)

```text
A simple worked example. Zara, a marketing executive in London, needs the busy sales team to log lead sources in the CRM, which they hate doing. She has no authority over them. She learns that the sales director is measured on pipeline growth, and the reps care about commission. So she shows them how better source data will help marketing send them more of the leads that close. She asks two respected reps to pilot a simpler two-click form for one month. They see more qualified leads and tell their colleagues. Adoption follows peer proof, not a memo.
```

Scene 8 — Worked example 2: Omar in Riyadh (illustrative)

```text
Now a realistic business scenario, illustrative. Omar is a data analyst at a telecom company in Riyadh. He believes a churn-prediction model could save the business money, but he's two levels below the commercial director who controls the budget. He maps her currencies: she's measured on retention this year and dislikes large, risky projects. So he proposes a small pilot on one customer segment, with a clear success measure. He asks his own manager to introduce the idea, and he brings a respected customer service manager who can speak to the problem. The director approves the pilot, and later sponsors the full project.
```

Scene 9 — Influencing senior leaders

```text
Influencing senior leaders needs a sharper format. They have little time and many demands. So lead with the recommendation, frame it in their terms, cost, risk, revenue or customers, and keep it to one page. Show options, including doing nothing. Be honest about risks. Offer a small, reversible first step. And build influence before you need it: share useful insights, help other teams, and make your manager look good. Finally, be ethical. Ask yourself: would I be comfortable if they knew exactly how I was trying to persuade them?
```

Scene 10 — Watch me: the influence plan

```text
Watch me do it. I'm planning to influence an illustrative head of operations to trial a new scheduling tool. I open the influence plan template. What I need: a thirty-day trial with one team, agreed by the end of the month. Her pressures: overtime costs and staff complaints. Her currencies: reduced overtime, and credit with her boss. Evidence she trusts: numbers from a similar team. Messenger: a shift lead she respects. Then I ask an AI assistant to list the three hardest questions a sceptical operations head might ask. Two are useful: data security and training time. I prepare honest answers to both.
```

Scene 11 — Measuring influence

```text
How do you measure influence? By outcomes and by relationships. Did you get the decision or the action? And just as important, is the relationship stronger afterwards? Do people come to you for advice? Are you invited to discussions earlier? A useful habit is to keep a simple log of your key asks: what you asked for, the outcome, and what you learned about that person's currencies. Over a year, you'll become far more effective, because you'll stop guessing what people value.
```

Scene 12 — Common mistakes

```text
Common mistakes. Leading with what you want instead of what they need. Relying on logic alone when trust is missing. Going over someone's head without trying them first. Asking for everything at once instead of a small first step. Only building relationships when you need something. Using persuasion techniques dishonestly, like inventing urgency. And letting an AI generate evidence or quotes. If it isn't real and sourced, it doesn't belong in a proposal.
```

Scene 13 — Recap and try this now

```text
Let's recap. Influence comes from expertise, relationships, reputation, information and reciprocity. Understand what people value and offer something genuine. Use persuasion principles honestly. Follow the seven-step plan, and ask for a small first step. For senior leaders, one page, their terms, honest options. Your try this now: pick one person whose support you need this quarter, complete the influence plan in the lesson, and make one genuine deposit in that relationship this week. Next, we'll apply these skills to one very important relationship: your own manager.
```

</details>

<details><summary>Narration per scene (paste into ElevenLabs)</summary>

Scene 1 — Managing up

```text
Here's a question. What does your manager worry about when they can't sleep? If you're not sure, you're not alone. Most of us spend a lot of energy on our own work and very little understanding the person whose support we need most. Managing up isn't flattery or office politics. It's building a working relationship with your manager so you both succeed. In this lecture, you'll learn how to understand your manager, agree expectations, keep them informed, disagree well, ask for what you need, and cope with a difficult manager.
```

Scene 2 — Why it matters

```text
Why does this matter? Because your manager influences almost everything about your working life: your priorities, your resources, your development, your visibility and often your next role. A good relationship means you get support earlier, your ideas get a fair hearing, and problems are solved before they grow. A poor one means surprises, frustration and missed opportunities on both sides. And remember: your manager has a manager too. Helping them succeed is part of how you succeed.
```

Scene 3 — Understand your manager

```text
Here's the concept. Start by understanding your manager as a person with their own goals and pressures. What are they measured on this year? What does their boss expect from them? How do they prefer to communicate: quick chat messages, detailed emails, or a weekly meeting? Do they want to be involved early in decisions or only hear about outcomes? How do they make decisions: data first, instinct, or peer views? Here's the key idea. Don't guess. Observe, and then ask directly. Most managers are glad to be asked.
```

Scene 4 — The co-driver

```text
Here's an analogy. Think of a rally driver and their co-driver. The co-driver doesn't drive, but they read the map, call out the bends early, and warn about hazards before they're visible. A good co-driver speaks in the format the driver needs, at the right moment. Too much talk is distracting. Too little is dangerous. Managing up works the same way. You help your manager navigate by calling out what's ahead, early and concisely, in the way they can use.
```

Scene 5 — Expectations and updates

```text
Next, agree expectations and keep your manager informed. Agree your top priorities, how success will be measured, how much autonomy you have on different kinds of decisions, and how often you'll meet. Write it down and check it every quarter. Then keep them informed without overwhelming them. A short weekly update works well: headline status, what's done, what's next, risks and what you need. And follow one golden rule: no surprises. Bad news early is a problem shared. Bad news late is a crisis, and a trust problem.
```

Scene 6 — Disagreeing well

```text
Now disagreement. Good managers want to hear concerns, as long as they're raised well. Do it privately, before the decision is final. Frame it around shared goals: I want this to succeed, so I want to share a concern. Bring evidence, not just feelings. Suggest an alternative or a way to reduce the risk. And then, if they still decide differently, support the decision fully, unless it's unethical, unsafe or illegal. In that case, use your organisation's speak-up routes. Asking for what you need works the same way: specific, in their terms, with a proposal.
```

Scene 7 — Worked example 1: Ahmed and Saima (illustrative)

```text
A simple worked example. Ahmed, an accountant at a firm in Lahore, feels his manager, Saima, keeps checking his work in too much detail. Instead of complaining, he asks her in their one-to-one: what would help you feel confident in the monthly reconciliations? She explains that a previous error embarrassed her in front of the partner. They agree a simple checklist and a two-line summary with each reconciliation. After two months, she stops checking line by line. Ahmed got autonomy by understanding her pressure, not by fighting it.
```

Scene 8 — Worked example 2: Aisha in Dubai (illustrative)

```text
Now a realistic business scenario, illustrative. Aisha is a project manager at a construction consultancy in Dubai. Her new director prefers detail by email, while her old one liked quick calls. Early on, she discovers a subcontractor delay that will push a milestone by two weeks. Instead of waiting until she has a fix, she emails him the same day: headline, impact, two options, and her recommendation. He picks her option and briefs the client himself before the client hears it elsewhere. Later, he tells her that was the moment he started trusting her judgement.
```

Scene 9 — A difficult manager

```text
What if your manager is genuinely difficult: disorganised, absent, or a micromanager? Start with curiosity. Is there a pressure behind the behaviour? Then adapt: for a disorganised manager, send short summaries of what you agreed. For an absent one, propose a regular fifteen-minute slot and bring a decision list. For a micromanager, over-communicate early to build confidence. Build a wider network of mentors and peers too. And if the behaviour becomes bullying, discriminatory or unethical, it's no longer a style issue. Document it and use HR or speak-up routes.
```

Scene 10 — Watch me: the weekly update

```text
Watch me do it. I'm writing an illustrative weekly update for my manager. Headline: at risk, because the vendor has delayed the data export by a week. Done this week: user testing complete, three fixes shipped. Next week: final training materials. Risks and asks: vendor delay, my plan is to move training earlier, and I need you to approve two hours of overtime by Wednesday. FYI: the client praised the new dashboard. It takes me five minutes. I read it once from my manager's point of view and delete one sentence that's only there to make me look busy.
```

Scene 11 — Measuring success

```text
How do you measure it? Your manager rarely surprised by you. Fewer revisions on your work. More autonomy over time, meaning more decisions at higher levels without checking in. Your manager bringing you into discussions earlier. And your development goals actually being discussed and supported. Once a quarter, ask: what's one thing I could do that would make your job easier? It's a powerful question, and very few people ever ask it.
```

Scene 12 — Common mistakes

```text
Common mistakes. Expecting your manager to read your mind. Waiting to be asked instead of offering updates. Hiding bad news until you've fixed it. Disagreeing in public meetings. Arriving with problems and no proposals. Complaining about your manager to their peers. And, the flip side, trying to please by saying yes to everything, then missing deadlines. Managing up is about honesty and partnership, not keeping your manager comfortable.
```

Scene 13 — Recap and try this now

```text
Let's recap. Understand your manager's goals, pressures and preferences, by observing and asking. Agree expectations in writing. Send concise updates, and never let them be surprised. Disagree privately with evidence and alternatives, then commit. Ask for what you need in their terms. With a difficult manager, adapt, build a network, and escalate genuine misconduct. Your try this now: fill in the working with my manager notes from the lesson, and send your first five-minute weekly update this Friday. Next module, we'll move to presentations and storytelling with data.
```

</details>

### 13. Structuring presentations that persuade

Lesson `structuring-presentations` · module “Presentations and storytelling with data” · target 8 min · 13 scenes · 1116 words · planned 8:04 (words ≈ 8:05) · status: **to produce**

| Minto | MIN-toe |

| 1 | 0:00 | 40 | Picture a senior leadership meeting. You have fifteen minutes and forty slides. By slide six, the chief executive is checking her phone. By slide twelve, she interrupts: sorry, what are you actually asking us for? That moment happens in boardrooms every day. The problem is rarely the slides. It's the structure. In this lecture, you'll learn to start with the audience and the ask, use proven structures like SCQA, put the executive summary first, design clean slides, and storyboard before you open any software. By the end, you'll have a seven-headline storyboard. | Structuring presentations<br>• Audience and ask<br>• Proven structures: SCQA<br>• Executive summary first<br>• Clean slides<br>• Storyboard first | Layout: a slide counter ticking from 1 to 40 in a corner. 0:00 slides flicker past; 0:10 at slide 6 a phone icon lights up on the table; 0:20 at slide 12 a speech bubble 'What are you asking us for?'; 0:35 agenda chips appear. |
| 2 | 0:40 | 30 | Why does this matter? Because presentations are often where decisions are made, budgets approved and reputations built. A well-structured presentation lets a busy audience understand your point in the first minute, and then spend the rest of the time on the questions that matter. A poorly structured one wastes everyone's time and can sink a good idea. And structure is a skill you can learn in an afternoon. | Why it matters<br>• Decisions, budgets, reputations<br>• Point in the first minute<br>• Time on the real questions<br>• Learnable in an afternoon | Layout: a timeline of a 15-minute meeting. 0:00 the good version shows a green 'point understood' marker at minute one and a long 'discussion' segment; 0:15 the poor version shows the point arriving at minute thirteen with no discussion time; 0:30 an afternoon clock icon. |
| 3 | 1:10 | 40 | Here's the first concept. Before you think about slides, answer two questions. Who is the audience: what do they know, what do they care about, and what are they worried about? And what's the ask: what do you want them to think, feel or do at the end? Everything in the presentation should serve that ask. If a slide doesn't move the audience towards it, cut it or move it to an appendix. Here's the key idea: a presentation isn't a report of everything you did. It's an argument for a decision. | Audience and ask<br>• What do they know, care about, fear?<br>• What should they think, feel, do?<br>• Every slide serves the ask<br>• An argument, not a report | Layout: a target with the word 'ask' at the centre. 0:00 slide thumbnails fly towards it; 0:15 those that hit the target stay; those that miss drop into an 'Appendix' tray; 0:35 caption 'argument, not report'. |
| 4 | 1:50 | 33 | Here's an analogy. Think of a satnav journey. Before you set off, the screen shows you the destination and the whole route. You know where you're going and roughly how long it'll take. Then it guides you turn by turn. Imagine a satnav that only revealed the destination when you arrived. That's what many presentations do, saving the recommendation for the last slide. Audiences relax when they know the destination. Tell them upfront, then take them there. | Show the destination first<br>• Satnav: destination + route first<br>• Then turn by turn<br>• Don't hide the recommendation<br>• Audiences relax when they know where you're going | Layout: a map with a route. 0:00 a destination pin appears first and the full route highlights; 0:15 a car follows turn by turn; 0:30 a second map where the pin only appears at the end, with confused question marks along the way. |
| 5 | 2:23 | 34 | Now proven structures. The most useful for business is SCQA, from Barbara Minto's pyramid principle. Situation: what the audience already accepts. Complication: what's changed or gone wrong. Question: the question that raises. Answer: your recommendation. Other structures work too: problem, solution, benefits; past, present, future; or options compared against criteria. Whichever you choose, put the executive summary first: the answer and the ask on slide one or two. Then the supporting evidence, then the risks, then the ask again. | Proven structures<br>• SCQA: Situation, Complication, Question, Answer<br>• Problem → solution → benefits<br>• Past → present → future<br>• Options vs criteria<br>• Executive summary first | Layout: four stacked blocks S, C, Q, A build from top to bottom, each with a one-line example; 0:30 three smaller alternative structure cards slide in from the right; 0:45 a slide labelled '1: Answer and ask' pins to the front of a deck. |
| 6 | 2:57 | 40 | Next, slide design. Use one message per slide, and write it as the slide title, as a full sentence. Not sales, but sales grew twelve per cent in the Gulf, driven by repeat orders. That's called an action title, and it means someone skimming just the titles gets your whole argument. Keep text minimal. Use visuals that prove the headline, such as a chart, a diagram or a before-and-after. Use consistent fonts and colours, generous white space, and large enough text to read at the back of the room or on a laptop. | Slide design<br>• One message per slide<br>• Action titles as full sentences<br>• Minimal text<br>• Visuals prove the headline<br>• Consistent, spacious, readable | Layout: a slide with the title 'Sales' rewrites as 'Sales grew 12% in the Gulf, driven by repeat orders'. 0:15 dense bullet text fades, replaced by one bar chart; 0:30 a white-space grid overlay; 0:40 a font-size check icon. |
| 7 | 3:37 | 38 | A simple worked example. Mariam is a team lead at a bank in Karachi. She wants approval for a new customer onboarding process. Her first draft opens with three slides on the history of onboarding. She restructures with SCQA. Situation: onboarding takes five days. Complication: two competitors now do it in one. Question: how do we close the gap without adding risk? Answer: a digital onboarding pilot for salaried customers. Slide one is the answer and the ask. Her director approves the pilot in the first ten minutes. | Worked example 1: Mariam in Karachi (illustrative)<br>• First draft: history slides<br>• SCQA: 5 days vs competitors' 1<br>• Answer: digital pilot<br>• Slide 1: answer + ask<br>• Approved in 10 minutes | Layout: a deck of slides reordering. 0:00 three 'history' slides at the front; 0:12 they slide to the appendix; 0:20 S, C, Q, A cards appear with Mariam's content; 0:40 slide one reads 'Pilot digital onboarding: approve today'; 0:50 a green 'Approved' stamp. |
| 8 | 4:15 | 44 | Now a realistic business scenario, illustrative. Ben is head of marketing at an online homeware brand in Leeds. He needs board approval for an illustrative budget of one hundred and twenty thousand pounds to relaunch the loyalty programme. He writes seven action titles first and reads them aloud to a colleague. She points out that slide four asserts that customers find rewards too slow, with no evidence. He adds exit-survey data. On the day, the board skims the titles in the pre-read, spends the meeting on the margin risk, and approves a capped budget. Structure turned a forty-minute debate into twenty. | Worked example 2: Ben in Leeds (illustrative)<br>• £120k loyalty relaunch<br>• Seven action titles first<br>• Read aloud: evidence gap found<br>• Board debates only the risk<br>• Capped budget approved | Layout: seven headline strips stacked vertically. 0:00 they type in; 0:20 strip four flashes amber with 'evidence?'; 0:30 a survey chart attaches to it; 0:45 a boardroom icon with a clock showing 20 minutes instead of 40; 0:55 a 'capped budget approved' card. |
| 9 | 4:59 | 36 | Different audiences need different emphasis. Executives want the answer, the options, the cost and the risk, in the fewest slides. Technical audiences want to see method and evidence, and they'll test your assumptions. Clients want to see themselves in the story: their problem, their outcome. Mixed audiences benefit from a short main deck and a well-labelled appendix. And for many internal decisions, consider sending a one-page pre-read instead of a long deck. The meeting then starts with questions rather than a performance. | Tailoring to audiences<br>• Executives: answer, options, cost, risk<br>• Technical: method, evidence<br>• Clients: their problem, their outcome<br>• Mixed: short deck + appendix<br>• Consider a one-page pre-read | Layout: three audience icons in a row: executive, engineer, client. 0:00 each gets a tailored deck thumbnail with different emphasis highlighted; 0:30 a single page icon labelled 'pre-read' slides beside a meeting-room icon. |
| 10 | 5:35 | 43 | Watch me do it. I storyboard an illustrative deck before opening any slide software. Audience: the operations director and finance. Ask: approve an October loyalty relaunch. <details><summary>Narration per scene (paste into ElevenLabs)</summary>

Scene 1 — Structuring presentations

```text
Picture a senior leadership meeting. You have fifteen minutes and forty slides. By slide six, the chief executive is checking her phone. By slide twelve, she interrupts: sorry, what are you actually asking us for? That moment happens in boardrooms every day. The problem is rarely the slides. It's the structure. In this lecture, you'll learn to start with the audience and the ask, use proven structures like SCQA, put the executive summary first, design clean slides, and storyboard before you open any software. By the end, you'll have a seven-headline storyboard.
```

Scene 2 — Why it matters

```text
Why does this matter? Because presentations are often where decisions are made, budgets approved and reputations built. A well-structured presentation lets a busy audience understand your point in the first minute, and then spend the rest of the time on the questions that matter. A poorly structured one wastes everyone's time and can sink a good idea. And structure is a skill you can learn in an afternoon.
```

Scene 3 — Audience and ask

```text
Here's the first concept. Before you think about slides, answer two questions. Who is the audience: what do they know, what do they care about, and what are they worried about? And what's the ask: what do you want them to think, feel or do at the end? Everything in the presentation should serve that ask. If a slide doesn't move the audience towards it, cut it or move it to an appendix. Here's the key idea: a presentation isn't a report of everything you did. It's an argument for a decision.
```

Scene 4 — Show the destination first

```text
Here's an analogy. Think of a satnav journey. Before you set off, the screen shows you the destination and the whole route. You know where you're going and roughly how long it'll take. Then it guides you turn by turn. Imagine a satnav that only revealed the destination when you arrived. That's what many presentations do, saving the recommendation for the last slide. Audiences relax when they know the destination. Tell them upfront, then take them there.
```

Scene 5 — Proven structures

```text
Now proven structures. The most useful for business is SCQA, from Barbara Minto's pyramid principle. Situation: what the audience already accepts. Complication: what's changed or gone wrong. Question: the question that raises. Answer: your recommendation. Other structures work too: problem, solution, benefits; past, present, future; or options compared against criteria. Whichever you choose, put the executive summary first: the answer and the ask on slide one or two. Then the supporting evidence, then the risks, then the ask again.
```

Scene 6 — Slide design

```text
Next, slide design. Use one message per slide, and write it as the slide title, as a full sentence. Not sales, but sales grew twelve per cent in the Gulf, driven by repeat orders. That's called an action title, and it means someone skimming just the titles gets your whole argument. Keep text minimal. Use visuals that prove the headline, such as a chart, a diagram or a before-and-after. Use consistent fonts and colours, generous white space, and large enough text to read at the back of the room or on a laptop.
```

Scene 7 — Worked example 1: Mariam in Karachi (illustrative)

```text
A simple worked example. Mariam is a team lead at a bank in Karachi. She wants approval for a new customer onboarding process. Her first draft opens with three slides on the history of onboarding. She restructures with SCQA. Situation: onboarding takes five days. Complication: two competitors now do it in one. Question: how do we close the gap without adding risk? Answer: a digital onboarding pilot for salaried customers. Slide one is the answer and the ask. Her director approves the pilot in the first ten minutes.
```

Scene 8 — Worked example 2: Ben in Leeds (illustrative)

```text
Now a realistic business scenario, illustrative. Ben is head of marketing at an online homeware brand in Leeds. He needs board approval for an illustrative budget of one hundred and twenty thousand pounds to relaunch the loyalty programme. He writes seven action titles first and reads them aloud to a colleague. She points out that slide four asserts that customers find rewards too slow, with no evidence. He adds exit-survey data. On the day, the board skims the titles in the pre-read, spends the meeting on the margin risk, and approves a capped budget. Structure turned a forty-minute debate into twenty.
```

Scene 9 — Tailoring to audiences

```text
Different audiences need different emphasis. Executives want the answer, the options, the cost and the risk, in the fewest slides. Technical audiences want to see method and evidence, and they'll test your assumptions. Clients want to see themselves in the story: their problem, their outcome. Mixed audiences benefit from a short main deck and a well-labelled appendix. And for many internal decisions, consider sending a one-page pre-read instead of a long deck. The meeting then starts with questions rather than a performance.
```

Scene 10 — Watch me: storyboard → outline

```text
Watch me do it. I storyboard an illustrative deck before opening any slide software. Audience: the operations director and finance. Ask: approve an October loyalty relaunch. I write SCQA in four lines, then seven action titles. I read the titles aloud. The argument holds. Then I paste the headlines into an AI assistant and ask for a seven-slide outline, keeping my headlines exactly, one suggested visual each, and flagging where I need data. It suggests a line chart for slide three and flags slide five as needing costs. Then I build the slides, and check every number against the source.
```

Scene 11 — Measuring success

```text
How do you measure success? Did you get the decision? How early in the meeting was your point understood? How much time went on the questions that mattered, rather than clarifying what you meant? After important presentations, ask one trusted attendee: what was the main message, and what was unclear? If their answer matches your slide one, your structure worked. If not, rework the structure before the next one, not the design.
```

Scene 12 — Common mistakes

```text
Common mistakes. Starting in the slide software instead of with the audience and the ask. Hiding the recommendation until the end. Slide titles that are labels, not messages. Walls of text you then read aloud. Too many slides for the time. Decorative charts that don't support the point. And using AI to generate a deck from nothing, which produces generic slides that look polished and say very little. Structure is your job. Tools only help you draw it.
```

Scene 13 — Recap and try this now

```text
Let's recap. Start with the audience and the ask. Use SCQA or another proven structure. Put the answer and the ask first. Write action titles, so the headlines alone tell the story. Design clean slides that prove each headline, and tailor to your audience. Storyboard before you build. Your try this now: for your next presentation, spend thirty minutes writing the SCQA and seven action titles from the lesson, and read the headlines aloud to someone before you open any slide software. Next, we'll look at how to tell honest, clear stories with data.
```

</details>

<details><summary>Narration per scene (paste into ElevenLabs)</summary>

Scene 1 — Storytelling with data

```text
Here's a chart. Twelve coloured lines, a legend with twelve entries, a title that says Q3 performance. Your audience squints, someone asks what the purple line is, and the meeting slides into a discussion of chart formatting. Sound familiar? Data doesn't speak for itself. People do. In this lecture, you'll learn to move from exploring data to explaining it, choose the right chart, declutter and focus attention, visualise honestly, and build a narrative around numbers. By the end, you'll transform one messy chart into a decision chart.
```

Scene 2 — Why it matters

```text
Why does this matter? Because decisions increasingly rely on data, and most people in a meeting won't do the analysis themselves. They depend on you to show them what matters. A clear chart with a clear message can turn a debate into a decision in minutes. A confusing or misleading one wastes time or, worse, drives the wrong decision. And with AI tools making charts in seconds, the scarce skill isn't making charts. It's choosing the right message and showing it honestly.
```

Scene 3 — Explore vs explain

```text
Here's the concept. Exploratory analysis is what you do to find the insight: slicing data, trying dozens of charts, following hunches. Explanatory analysis is what you show an audience: the one or two charts that prove your point. Many people present their exploration, all the charts they made. Your audience doesn't need your journey. Here's the key idea: before you make a chart for others, write one sentence. The audience needs to decide this, and the insight that matters is that. Then build the chart to prove that sentence.
```

Scene 4 — The detective's wall

```text
Here's an analogy. Think of a detective in a crime drama. During the investigation, there's a wall covered in photos, maps and string. That's exploration. But when the detective explains the case in the final scene, they don't walk everyone through the whole wall. They point to three pieces of evidence that prove who did it, in order. Your data story is the final scene. Show the evidence that proves the conclusion, and keep the wall in your appendix.
```

Scene 5 — Choosing and decluttering

```text
Choosing the right chart is simpler than people think. Change over time: a line chart. Comparing categories: a horizontal bar chart, sorted from biggest to smallest. Parts of a whole: a stacked bar or a simple percentage bar. Pie charts are hard to read beyond two or three slices. Relationship between two measures: a scatter plot. And one important number: just show it big, with context, like up from last quarter. Then declutter. Remove gridlines, borders and 3D. Label data directly instead of using a legend. Grey everything, then colour only what proves your point.
```

Scene 6 — Honest visualisation

```text
Honesty in visualisation is non-negotiable. Bar charts should start at zero, because the length of the bar is the message. Truncating the axis can make a two per cent change look like a doubling. Line charts can use a narrower range, but make it clear. Don't cherry-pick date ranges to hide an inconvenient trend. Show uncertainty where it matters. And be careful with averages that hide important differences between groups. If your chart could be accused of misleading, it will be. And your credibility is worth more than any single argument.
```

Scene 7 — Worked example 1: Fatima's bakery (illustrative)

```text
A simple worked example. Fatima runs a small online bakery in Islamabad. She wants to show her business partner that weekend orders are growing much faster than weekday orders. Her first chart is a table of fifty-two weeks. She writes her sentence: we should add a second baker on Saturdays, because weekend orders have grown much faster. She makes a line chart with two lines, weekend in her accent colour and weekday in grey, labels them directly, and titles it: weekend orders have doubled since January, weekday orders are flat. Her partner agrees in one conversation.
```

Scene 8 — Worked example 2: Kareem in Riyadh (illustrative)

```text
Now a realistic business scenario, illustrative. Kareem is an analyst at a regional airline based in Riyadh. Leadership is debating whether to add capacity on a route. The dashboard has thirty charts. Kareem builds a data story in three charts. First, load factor on the route over two years: consistently high. Second, lost bookings from sold-out flights, estimated from search data, labelled with its assumptions. Third, the illustrative revenue impact of adding one weekly flight, with a range, not a single number. The action titles tell the story. Leadership approves a trial for one season.
```

Scene 9 — Numbers people can grasp

```text
Numbers need translation. People struggle with large numbers, percentages of percentages and abstract units. So give comparisons. That's about one customer in every five. That's roughly the cost of two extra staff for a year. Round sensibly. Nobody needs twenty-seven point four three per cent in a presentation; about twenty-seven per cent is fine. And tell the story in three beats: here's where we were, here's what changed, here's what we should do. That's the SCQA structure from the last lesson, applied to data.
```

Scene 10 — Watch me: messy data → decision chart

```text
Watch me do it. I take an illustrative CSV of monthly repeat-order rates. I write my sentence first: we need to fix the loyalty programme before peak season, because repeat orders have fallen every month. In a spreadsheet, I insert a line chart, remove the gridlines, start the axis at zero, and label only January and June. Title: repeat-order rate has fallen every month since January. Then I ask an AI assistant with data analysis to describe the trend and show its calculations. It claims a seven-point fall. I check in the sheet: correct. I also ask it for caveats. It notes seasonality. I add that to my notes.
```

Scene 11 — Measuring success

```text
How do you measure success with data stories? Did the audience reach a decision? Did they ask about the implications, rather than what the chart means? Could someone repeat your headline after the meeting? And did anyone later find a flaw you should have spotted? Ask a colleague to look at your chart for five seconds, then tell you what it says. If they can't, simplify it. That five-second test is one of the most useful habits you can build.
```

Scene 12 — Common mistakes

```text
Common mistakes. Presenting exploration instead of explanation. No headline, just a label. Too many colours and categories. Pie charts with ten slices. Truncated axes on bar charts. False precision. Dashboards used as presentations. And trusting AI-generated numbers or charts without checking the calculation. AI analysis tools are useful, but they can misread columns or make arithmetic errors. You're accountable for every number on your slide.
```

Scene 13 — Recap and try this now

```text
Let's recap. Separate exploring from explaining. Write your one-sentence message first. Choose the simplest chart that proves it, declutter, and use colour to focus attention. Visualise honestly. Translate numbers into things people can grasp, and tell the story in three beats. Use AI for analysis, then verify every number. Your try this now: take one chart you've presented recently and remake it using the five steps in the lesson, then run the five-second test on a colleague. Next, we'll work on delivery and handling questions.
```

</details>

By the end, you'll have a rehearsal plan and a Q&A grid. | Delivery and Q&A<br>• Prepare and rehearse<br>• Voice and body<br>• Manage nerves<br>• Answer structure<br>• Difficult questions<br>• Online delivery | Layout: a stage with a lectern. 0:00 a presenter silhouette steps up; 0:08 a brief blank-screen flash; 0:15 a speed indicator spikes; 0:25 a hand raises from the audience with 'your numbers are wrong?'; 0:35 agenda chips appear. |
| 2 | 0:42 | 29 | Why does this matter? Because audiences judge the message partly by the messenger. Confident, clear delivery makes people more willing to trust your analysis. It's where senior people test whether you truly understand your proposal. The good news is that confident delivery is mostly preparation, not personality. | Why it matters<br>• The messenger shapes the message<br>• Confidence → trust<br>• Q&A is where decisions are tested<br>• Mostly preparation, not personality | Layout: two identical slides presented by two silhouettes. 0:00 left presenter calm, audience nods; 0:12 right presenter rushed, audience frowns; 0:25 a spotlight moves to a Q&A icon labelled 'where decisions happen'; 0:35 caption 'preparation, not personality'. |
