<details><summary>Narration per scene (paste into ElevenLabs)</summary>

Scene 1 — Delivery and Q&A

```text
You've got a strong structure and clean slides. Then you stand up, your mind goes blank for a second, you speak too fast, and the first question comes from the finance director who clearly thinks your numbers are wrong. Delivery is where good presentations are won or lost. In this lecture, you'll learn how to prepare and rehearse, how to use your voice and body, how to manage nerves, a simple structure for answering questions, how to handle difficult questions, and how to present online. By the end, you'll have a rehearsal plan and a Q&A grid.
```

Scene 2 — Why it matters

```text
Why does this matter? Because audiences judge the message partly by the messenger. Confident, clear delivery makes people more willing to trust your analysis. Hesitant, rushed or defensive delivery can undermine even excellent content. And Q&A is often where decisions are really made. It's where senior people test whether you truly understand your proposal. The good news is that confident delivery is mostly preparation, not personality.
```

Scene 3 — Rehearse out loud

```text
Here's the concept: rehearse out loud, not in your head. Mental rehearsal skips the hard parts: transitions, timing and the moment you have to explain a complex chart in plain words. A simple protocol is three runs. Run one: out loud, alone, full length, and time it. Run two: record yourself on video. Watch once with the sound off for body language, and once with sound only for pace and filler words. Run three: present to one colleague and ask what they took away. Here's the key idea. Three runs out loud will transform most presentations.
```

Scene 4 — The flight simulator

```text
Here's an analogy. Think of a pilot's pre-flight routine. Experienced pilots don't rely on feeling confident. They follow a checklist, practise emergencies in a simulator, and know exactly what they'll do if an engine fails. That's why they stay calm when something goes wrong. Your rehearsal and Q&A grid are your simulator. When the difficult question comes, you've already flown through it. Confidence isn't the absence of nerves; it's knowing what you'll do next.
```

Scene 5 — Voice and body

```text
Now voice and body. Slow down: nerves speed us up, so aim for a pace that feels slightly slow to you. Use pauses, especially after a key point. A two-second pause feels long to you and powerful to the audience. Vary your tone to stress important words. Stand or sit steadily, with open gestures. Look at people, one person per thought, rather than at the slides. And use the slides as a prompt, not a script. If you're reading the slide, the audience will read ahead of you and stop listening.
```

Scene 6 — Managing nerves

```text
Managing nerves. First, nerves are normal. Even very experienced presenters feel them. The physical feeling of anxiety and excitement is very similar, so some people find it helps to tell themselves, I'm excited, rather than trying to calm down. Practical steps help too. Arrive early and test the technology. Know your first two minutes almost word for word, because that's when nerves peak. Use slow breathing: breathe out for longer than you breathe in. And remember the audience wants you to succeed. They're not waiting for you to fail.
```

Scene 7 — Answering questions

```text
Now answering questions. Use a simple structure: answer, reason, end. Answer in one sentence. Give the reason or the evidence. Then end, and check: does that answer your question? Listen to the whole question first, and pause before answering. If it's unclear, ask a clarifying question. If you don't know, say so, and commit to finding out by a specific time. Never bluff. Senior audiences are very good at spotting it, and it damages trust in everything else you've said.
```

Scene 8 — Worked example 1: Daniyal in Lahore (illustrative)

```text
A simple worked example. Daniyal, a junior analyst in Lahore, is presenting a pricing proposal to the managing director. The first question is: why should we believe customers will pay more? He pauses, then answers: because in our pilot, most customers accepted the new price. The reason: here's the conversion data on slide eight, with the sample size. And ends: does that answer your question? The managing director nods and asks about rollout. Daniyal had put exactly that question in his Q&A grid the night before.
```

Scene 9 — Worked example 2: Sophie in London (illustrative)

```text
Now a realistic business scenario, illustrative, with a difficult question. Sophie, a project director at an engineering firm in London, presents a delayed project update to a client's board. A board member says sharply: this is the third delay, why should we trust your new date? Sophie doesn't get defensive. She acknowledges: you're right to ask, three delays is not acceptable. She answers: the new date is based on a completed design, not an estimate. She gives the evidence and the two risks remaining. And she offers a fortnightly progress report. The board accepts the plan. Acknowledge, answer, evidence, commitment.
```

Scene 10 — Presenting online

```text
Presenting online needs extra care. Put your camera at eye level and light in front of you. Test your microphone and screen sharing beforehand. Close notifications. Because you can't read the room, pause for questions every five to seven minutes, and name people when inviting input. Keep a backup: a PDF of the deck ready to share if screen sharing fails. And in hybrid sessions, speak to the camera as well as the room, and check the chat regularly, or ask a colleague to monitor it for you.
```

Scene 11 — Watch me: the Q&A grid

```text
Watch me do it. I fill in the Q&A grid for an illustrative budget presentation. I list five likely questions. For the finance director: what if the margin impact is bigger? Short answer: we cap it and review monthly. Evidence: appendix slide A3. For operations: who does the extra work? Answer: no extra headcount in phase one; the evidence is the process map. Then I paste my key points into an AI assistant and ask it to act as a sceptical finance director and ask five hard questions. Two are new. I add them to the grid, with honest answers.
```

Scene 12 — Measuring delivery

```text
How do you measure delivery? Record yourself and compare over time: pace, filler words and eye contact. After each presentation, note which questions came up and whether they were in your grid. Aim for most of them to be. Ask a trusted colleague for one thing to keep and one thing to change. And look at the outcome: did the audience make the decision, and did the Q&A strengthen your case or weaken it?
```

Scene 13 — Common mistakes

```text
Common mistakes. Rehearsing only in your head. Reading slides aloud. Rushing, especially at the start. Apologising for being nervous. Defending instead of listening when challenged. Bluffing answers. Running over time, which eats the Q&A. And online, sharing the wrong screen or talking for twenty minutes with no interaction. Each of these is fixed by preparation, not talent.
```

Scene 14 — Recap and try this now

```text
Let's recap. Rehearse out loud three times, including one video recording. Slow down, pause and look at people. Treat nerves as energy, and know your first two minutes. Answer questions with answer, reason, end. Acknowledge hostile questions before answering, and never bluff. Online, check your setup and build in interaction. Your try this now: for your next presentation, complete the three-run rehearsal and fill in the Q&A grid in the lesson with at least five questions. Next module, we'll look at leading remote, hybrid and multicultural teams, and guiding people through change.
```

</details>

### 16. Leading remote and hybrid teams

Teams that design how they work often outperform co-located teams, because they're deliberate. Teams that don't end up with confusion, long hours and people who feel invisible. | Why it matters<br>• Distributed work is normal<br>• Office defaults don't travel<br>• Design it deliberately<br>• Undesigned = confusion, long hours, invisibility | Layout: an office floor plan where overheard-conversation lines connect desks. 0:00 lines glow; 0:15 the desks drift apart across a map and the lines break; 0:30 new deliberate lines form labelled 'working agreement', 'cadence', 'docs'. |
| 3 | 1:15 | 38 | Here's the concept. Four principles for distributed teams. First, write things down: decisions, context and processes live in shared documents, not in people's heads. Second, default to asynchronous work where you can, and use meetings for what really needs real-time conversation, like building relationships, resolving conflict or complex problem-solving. Third, be explicit about expectations: response times, availability and how decisions are made. And fourth, judge by outcomes, not by online status or hours visible on camera. Here's the key idea: in a distributed team, clarity replaces proximity. | Four principles<br>• Write it down<br>• Async by default<br>• Explicit expectations<br>• Outcomes, not online status<br>• Clarity replaces proximity | Layout: four pillar icons rise in turn: document, clock with arrows, contract, target. 0:40 a banner across the top 'Clarity replaces proximity'. |
| 4 | 1:53 | 35 | Here's an analogy. Think of a relay team compared with a football team. Footballers can shout to each other in real time and adjust instantly. A relay team can't. So they practise the handover obsessively: exactly where, exactly how, the signal, the grip. A distributed team is more like a relay team. The work passes between people in different places and time zones. The quality of the handover, a clear message, a well-written document, a recorded explanation, determines the result. | Relay, not football<br>• Football: real-time adjustments<br>• Relay: rehearsed handovers<br>• Distributed teams live on handovers<br>• Clear messages, docs, recordings | Layout: split screen. 0:00 left, football players passing and shouting; 0:12 right, relay runners with a baton; 0:25 the baton handover slows down to show a clear zone and grip; 0:40 the baton morphs into a document icon. |
| 5 | 2:28 | 38 | Now the working agreement: the single most useful tool for a distributed team. Write it together. It covers core overlap hours, which channel is used for what and the expected response time for each, meeting norms, how status is shared, how decisions are recorded, protected focus time, time-off expectations, and which AI tools are approved and with what data. Then set a communication cadence. For example, a weekly written update, one short live team meeting, fortnightly one-to-ones, and a quarterly retrospective where you review the agreement itself. | The working agreement + cadence<br>• Overlap hours<br>• Channels and response times<br>• Meeting norms, status, decisions<br>• Focus time, time off, AI tools<br>• Cadence: weekly update, team call, 1:1s, quarterly retro | Layout: a document titled 'Team working agreement' fills section by section with small icons; 0:35 a calendar strip below shows repeating blocks: weekly update, weekly call, fortnightly 1:1s, quarterly retro. |
| 6 | 3:06 | 37 | Trust at a distance needs deliberate effort. You don't bump into people, so create moments for connection: a few minutes of non-work conversation at the start of calls, virtual coffee pairings, and meeting in person when budgets allow, especially at the start of a project. Be reliable and responsive. Share context generously, because people fill information gaps with worst-case assumptions. And be careful with surveillance tools that track keystrokes or screens. They signal distrust, can raise legal issues, and usually measure activity rather than results. | Trust at a distance<br>• Create connection moments<br>• Meet in person when you can<br>• Be reliable and responsive<br>• Share context generously<br>• Avoid surveillance tools | Layout: a video-call grid. 0:00 tiles show small coffee cups; 0:12 a pairing animation connects two tiles; 0:25 a 'context' document flows to all tiles; 0:40 a keystroke-tracker icon is crossed out with labels 'distrust' and 'activity, not results'. |
| 7 | 3:43 | 35 | A simple worked example. Hamza leads a four-person design team split between Karachi and Manchester. Messages fly at all hours and people feel always on. He runs a one-hour session where the team writes a working agreement. They set a three-hour overlap window, agree that chat outside those hours gets a reply the next working day, and create a weekly written update on Thursdays. Within a month, late-night messages drop sharply, and designers say they finally have time to focus. | Worked example 1: Hamza's team (illustrative)<br>• Karachi + Manchester<br>• 'Always on'<br>• Three-hour overlap<br>• Next-day reply outside overlap<br>• Thursday written update | Layout: a 24-hour clock ring with two time zones. 0:00 message dots scattered all around the ring; 0:15 a three-hour overlap segment highlights; 0:30 dots cluster inside the overlap; 0:45 a 'focus time' block appears outside it. |
| 8 | 4:18 | 40 | Now a realistic business scenario, illustrative. Noor leads a twenty-person customer success team for a software company, with people in Riyadh, Dubai, Cairo and London. In a hybrid set-up, the Dubai office staff get quick answers and interesting projects because they're near the director. Remote colleagues feel sidelined. Noor introduces rules: all decisions recorded in a shared log, everyone joins meetings from their own device, stretch projects advertised to the whole team, and meeting times rotated. After two quarters, engagement scores among remote members rise and two remote colleagues lead major accounts. | Worked example 2: Noor's team (illustrative)<br>• Riyadh, Dubai, Cairo, London<br>• Proximity bias in the hub office<br>• Decision log, own-device meetings<br>• Stretch projects advertised<br>• Rotated meeting times | Layout: a map with four city pins; 0:00 Dubai glows brighter with arrows of opportunity pointing to it; 0:20 four fairness rules appear as cards; 0:40 opportunity arrows redistribute evenly; 0:55 two remote pins gain 'account lead' badges. |
| 9 | 4:58 | 36 | Fairness and wellbeing need your attention in hybrid teams. Proximity bias is real: people who are physically near leaders tend to get more information, more visibility and more opportunities. Counter it deliberately. And protect wellbeing. Remote work can blur boundaries, so model healthy habits: don't send messages late at night that imply urgency, respect weekends in each country, which may differ, and encourage real time off. <details><summary>Narration per scene (paste into ElevenLabs)</summary>

Scene 1 — Leading remote and hybrid teams

```text
It's nine at night in Lahore. Your developer there has just seen a message you sent at four in the afternoon from London, marked urgent. She doesn't know whether to reply now or wait. Meanwhile, your colleague in Dubai is wondering why nobody answered his question from this morning. Nobody did anything wrong. The team just never agreed how to work. In this lecture, you'll learn the principles of leading distributed teams, how to write a team working agreement, set a communication cadence, build trust at a distance, keep hybrid work fair, and protect wellbeing.
```

Scene 2 — Why it matters

```text
Why does this matter? Because distributed work is now normal for many teams, whether fully remote, hybrid, or spread across offices and countries. What used to happen naturally in an office, quick questions, overheard context, spotting that someone's struggling, doesn't happen by default at a distance. You have to design it. Teams that design how they work often outperform co-located teams, because they're deliberate. Teams that don't end up with confusion, long hours and people who feel invisible.
```

Scene 3 — Four principles

```text
Here's the concept. Four principles for distributed teams. First, write things down: decisions, context and processes live in shared documents, not in people's heads. Second, default to asynchronous work where you can, and use meetings for what really needs real-time conversation, like building relationships, resolving conflict or complex problem-solving. Third, be explicit about expectations: response times, availability and how decisions are made. And fourth, judge by outcomes, not by online status or hours visible on camera. Here's the key idea: in a distributed team, clarity replaces proximity.
```

Scene 4 — Relay, not football

```text
Here's an analogy. Think of a relay team compared with a football team. Footballers can shout to each other in real time and adjust instantly. A relay team can't. So they practise the handover obsessively: exactly where, exactly how, the signal, the grip. A distributed team is more like a relay team. The work passes between people in different places and time zones. The quality of the handover, a clear message, a well-written document, a recorded explanation, determines the result.
```

Scene 5 — The working agreement + cadence

```text
Now the working agreement: the single most useful tool for a distributed team. Write it together. It covers core overlap hours, which channel is used for what and the expected response time for each, meeting norms, how status is shared, how decisions are recorded, protected focus time, time-off expectations, and which AI tools are approved and with what data. Then set a communication cadence. For example, a weekly written update, one short live team meeting, fortnightly one-to-ones, and a quarterly retrospective where you review the agreement itself.
```

Scene 6 — Trust at a distance

```text
Trust at a distance needs deliberate effort. You don't bump into people, so create moments for connection: a few minutes of non-work conversation at the start of calls, virtual coffee pairings, and meeting in person when budgets allow, especially at the start of a project. Be reliable and responsive. Share context generously, because people fill information gaps with worst-case assumptions. And be careful with surveillance tools that track keystrokes or screens. They signal distrust, can raise legal issues, and usually measure activity rather than results.
```

Scene 7 — Worked example 1: Hamza's team (illustrative)

```text
A simple worked example. Hamza leads a four-person design team split between Karachi and Manchester. Messages fly at all hours and people feel always on. He runs a one-hour session where the team writes a working agreement. They set a three-hour overlap window, agree that chat outside those hours gets a reply the next working day, and create a weekly written update on Thursdays. Within a month, late-night messages drop sharply, and designers say they finally have time to focus.
```

Scene 8 — Worked example 2: Noor's team (illustrative)

```text
Now a realistic business scenario, illustrative. Noor leads a twenty-person customer success team for a software company, with people in Riyadh, Dubai, Cairo and London. In a hybrid set-up, the Dubai office staff get quick answers and interesting projects because they're near the director. Remote colleagues feel sidelined. Noor introduces rules: all decisions recorded in a shared log, everyone joins meetings from their own device, stretch projects advertised to the whole team, and meeting times rotated. After two quarters, engagement scores among remote members rise and two remote colleagues lead major accounts.
```

Scene 9 — Fairness and wellbeing

```text
Fairness and wellbeing need your attention in hybrid teams. Proximity bias is real: people who are physically near leaders tend to get more information, more visibility and more opportunities. Counter it deliberately. And protect wellbeing. Remote work can blur boundaries, so model healthy habits: don't send messages late at night that imply urgency, respect weekends in each country, which may differ, and encourage real time off. Some countries also have legal rules on working time and the right to disconnect, so check yours.
```

Scene 10 — Watch me: the working agreement

```text
Watch me do it. I draft an illustrative working agreement for a team across London, Dubai and Lahore. First, the overlap: I calculate three hours when all three are at work, and I remember that daylight saving in the UK shifts it for part of the year. Channels: urgent means a call, chat gets a same-day reply, email two days. Focus time: Tuesday and Thursday mornings. AI tools: our approved assistant only, no client data elsewhere, meeting notes announced and checked. Then I share it as a draft and ask the team to comment by Friday. It's theirs, not mine.
```

Scene 11 — Measuring success

```text
How do you measure success? Watch a few signals. Are people clear on priorities without asking? Are response-time expectations met without late-night messages? Is information equal for remote and office staff? What do engagement or pulse surveys say, split by location? And are delivery outcomes steady or improving? Ask in your quarterly retrospective: what's one thing in our working agreement that isn't working? Then change it. The agreement is a living document.
```

Scene 12 — Common mistakes

```text
Common mistakes. Recreating the office online with back-to-back video calls. Expecting instant replies at all hours. Making decisions in corridor conversations that remote people never hear about. Measuring presence instead of outcomes. Using surveillance software instead of trust. Scheduling every meeting at times convenient for headquarters. Forgetting that weekends and holidays differ across countries. And rolling out AI notetakers or assistants without agreeing the rules.
```

Scene 13 — Recap and try this now

```text
Let's recap. In distributed teams, clarity replaces proximity. Write things down, default to async, be explicit, and judge outcomes. Create a working agreement with your team, and a regular cadence. Build trust deliberately, counter proximity bias, and protect wellbeing. Your try this now: use the template in the lesson to draft your team's working agreement, share it as a draft with a comment deadline, and run the fairness check at the end of the month. Next, we'll look at communicating across cultures.
```

</details>

<details><summary>Narration per scene (paste into ElevenLabs)</summary>

Scene 1 — Cross-cultural communication

```text
A British manager says to her new colleague in Riyadh: that's an interesting idea, perhaps we could think about it a little more. She means: no. He hears: she likes it and wants more detail. He spends two days developing it. Then there's awkwardness on both sides. Nobody was rude. They were just speaking different cultural languages, in the same English. In this lecture, you'll learn how culture shapes communication, useful dimensions to understand differences, practical habits for multicultural teams, language and fairness, and how to build cultural intelligence.
```

Scene 2 — Why it matters

```text
Why does this matter? Because many teams now include people from several countries, whether in one office or spread across time zones. Think of teams spanning Pakistan, the UAE, Saudi Arabia, the UK and the US. Misunderstandings about feedback, deadlines, disagreement and decisions cause friction, slow delivery and damaged trust. And they often go unspoken, because each person assumes their own way is normal. Leaders who can bridge these differences get the full value of a diverse team: more perspectives and better decisions.
```

Scene 3 — Useful dimensions (e.g. Meyer's Culture Map)

```text
Here's the concept. Researchers have described dimensions along which cultures tend to differ. One widely used framework is Erin Meyer's culture map, with scales like communicating, from low-context, explicit and literal, to high-context, where meaning is implied. Evaluating: direct versus indirect negative feedback. Deciding: consensual or top-down. Trusting: task-based or relationship-based. Disagreeing: open debate or avoiding confrontation. And scheduling: linear or flexible time. Here's the key idea. These describe tendencies across groups, not individuals. Use them to form questions, never stereotypes.
```

Scene 4 — Rules of the road

```text
Here's an analogy. Think of cultural norms as the rules of the road. In the UK, people drive on the left; in the UAE and Saudi Arabia, on the right. Neither is correct. They're just conventions. A driver who crosses borders doesn't declare the other side wrong. They notice, slow down, and adapt. And a sensible driver doesn't assume that every driver in a country behaves identically either. Cross-cultural skill is like driving abroad well: notice the conventions, adapt carefully, and watch the actual car in front of you.
```

Scene 5 — Practical habits

```text
Now practical habits. Make the implicit explicit. Say clearly whether a deadline is fixed or flexible, and whether you're making a decision or asking for input. Check understanding by asking people to summarise, not by asking, is that clear? Because yes can mean I heard you, not I agree. Create several ways to contribute, such as written input before meetings, because not everyone will speak up in a group. Learn about the working days, public holidays and religious observances in your team. And adapt feedback: private and specific works well across most cultures.
```

Scene 6 — Language and fairness

```text
Language and fairness matter too. In most global teams, English is the working language, and for many people it's a second or third language. Native speakers often speak too fast and use idioms: let's circle back, ballpark figure, it's not cricket. Slow down, use plain words, and put important points in writing. Don't mistake fluency for competence, or an accent for a lack of expertise. Machine translation and live captions can help, but check anything important with a fluent colleague, and never rely on them for contracts, HR matters or safety instructions.
```

Scene 7 — Worked example 1: Emily and the Lahore team (illustrative)

```text
A simple worked example. Emily, a project manager in Manchester, notices her team in Lahore never raises problems in the weekly call, but deadlines still slip. She stops asking, any issues? in the group call. Instead, she asks each person to send a short written update before the meeting with a red, amber or green status and one risk. She also has short one-to-ones. Risks start appearing a week earlier. It wasn't that the team had no concerns. The format didn't make it comfortable to raise them in front of the group.
```

Scene 8 — Worked example 2: US–Riyadh partnership (illustrative)

```text
Now a realistic business scenario, illustrative. A US software company is partnering with a family-owned distributor in Riyadh. The US team sends a detailed contract draft in the first week and pushes for a signature within ten days. The partner goes quiet. The US account lead, Chris, asks a colleague with regional experience, who explains that the partner expects to build the relationship first, and that decisions sit with the senior family member. Chris travels for an in-person meeting, spends time on the relationship, and adjusts the timeline. The deal closes a month later, on better terms for both sides.
```

Scene 9 — Cultural intelligence (CQ)

```text
Cultural intelligence, often called CQ, from the work of Christopher Earley and Soon Ang, describes the capability to work effectively across cultures. It has four parts. Drive: your motivation to adapt. Knowledge: understanding of how cultures differ. Strategy: planning and checking your assumptions. And action: adapting your behaviour appropriately. The good news is that it can be developed. The habits in this lesson build it, especially asking rather than assuming, and reflecting after interactions that surprised you.
```

Scene 10 — Watch me: culture-map session

```text
Watch me do it. I'm preparing an illustrative team culture-map session for a team spanning London, Dubai and Karachi. I list the six scales from the lesson. Each person rates themselves privately in a simple online form, so nobody anchors on others. Then I show the spread anonymously. The biggest gap is in feedback: some prefer direct, public comments, others strongly prefer private. We agree a norm: feedback is private and specific, and praise can be public if the person is comfortable. I add it to our working agreement.
```

Scene 11 — Measuring success

```text
How do you measure success? Fewer misunderstandings about deadlines and decisions. More even contribution in meetings and documents across nationalities and first languages. Risks raised early from every location. And, in team surveys, people from every background saying they feel respected and able to speak up. You can also reflect personally after each important cross-cultural interaction: what surprised me? What would I do differently? That reflection builds your cultural intelligence over time.
```

Scene 12 — Common mistakes

```text
Common mistakes. Assuming your own norms are normal. Stereotyping individuals based on nationality. Taking yes as agreement. Treating silence as having nothing to say. Idioms and sarcasm in writing. Scheduling around only one country's holidays. Judging competence by fluency or accent. And relying on machine translation for sensitive or high-stakes messages without checking.
```

Scene 13 — Recap and try this now

```text
Let's recap. Culture shapes how we communicate, give feedback, decide, trust, disagree and schedule. Use frameworks as questions, not stereotypes. Make the implicit explicit, check understanding by summarising, offer several ways to contribute, and be fair about language. Build cultural intelligence through curiosity and reflection. Your try this now: run the team culture-map session in the lesson, or ask each person to write a how I work profile, and agree one team norm from the biggest gap. Next, we'll look at leading people through change.
```

</details>

### 18. Leading people through change

Lesson `leading-through-change` · module “Remote, hybrid and cross-cultural leadership” · target 8 min · 13 scenes · 1138 words · planned 8:16 (words ≈ 8:15) · status: **to produce**

| Kotter | KOT-er |

| 1 | 0:00 | 41 | Think about the last big change at work: a new system, a restructure, a new way of working, perhaps AI tools arriving in your team. How did people react? Some were enthusiastic. Some were anxious. Some said nothing and quietly carried on the old way. That's normal. Change isn't just a project plan; it's an emotional experience for the people living through it. In this lecture, you'll learn models that help you understand change, how to diagnose where people are stuck with ADKAR, how to communicate change, support people through transition, and measure adoption. | Leading through change<br>• Change is emotional<br>• Helpful models<br>• Diagnose with ADKAR<br>• Communicate<br>• Support and measure adoption | Layout: a workplace scene with a large 'New system' banner. 0:00 banner unfurls; 0:10 three groups of icons react: cheering, worried, quietly walking the old path; 0:25 an 'emotional experience' heart icon pulses; 0:35 agenda chips appear. |
| 2 | 0:41 | 32 | Why does this matter? Because many change initiatives fail to deliver what they promised, and the reason is rarely the technology or the plan. It's that people don't adopt the change, or adopt it half-heartedly. A new system nobody uses properly is just an expensive system. Leaders who manage the human side of change get faster adoption, less disruption and keep people's trust for the next change. And there's always a next change. | Why it matters<br>• Changes often fail on adoption<br>• Unused systems are costly<br>• Human side = faster adoption<br>• Trust for the next change | Layout: an expensive-looking dashboard icon covered in cobwebs labelled 'unused'. 0:00 it appears; 0:15 an adoption curve rises steeply when a 'people' icon is added; 0:30 a series of future change icons queue up. |
| 3 | 1:13 | 40 | Here's the concept. Three models help. John Kotter's eight steps focus on leading organisational change: urgency, a guiding coalition, a vision, communication, removing obstacles, quick wins, building on change and anchoring it in culture. William Bridges' transition model says that change is the external event, but transition is the internal, psychological process: an ending, a neutral zone of confusion, then a new beginning. And Prosci's ADKAR model describes what each individual needs: awareness, desire, knowledge, ability and reinforcement. Here's the key idea: organisations don't change; people do, one at a time. | Models that help<br>• Kotter: 8 steps<br>• Bridges: ending → neutral zone → new beginning<br>• ADKAR: awareness, desire, knowledge, ability, reinforcement<br>• People change one at a time | Layout: three model cards appear in sequence. 0:00 Kotter's eight steps as a staircase; 0:20 Bridges as three overlapping curves; 0:35 ADKAR as five stepping stones; 0:50 caption 'people change one at a time'. |
| 4 | 1:53 | 40 | Here's an analogy. Think of moving house. The move itself happens on one day: that's the change. But the transition takes months. There's the ending: leaving a home full of memories, even if the new one is better. Then the neutral zone: boxes everywhere, you can't find the kettle, your commute is unfamiliar. Then the new beginning: the new place finally feels like home. A good leader in change is like a good friend when you move house. They acknowledge the loss, help you through the boxes, and celebrate when you're settled. | Moving house<br>• Move day = the change<br>• Ending: leaving the old home<br>• Neutral zone: boxes, lost kettle<br>• New beginning: it feels like home | Layout: three panels. 0:00 a house with a 'sold' sign and a wistful figure; 0:15 a room full of boxes and a missing-kettle question mark; 0:30 a cosy room with a kettle steaming; 0:45 caption 'acknowledge, help, celebrate'. |
| 5 | 2:33 | 38 | Now ADKAR as a diagnostic. For each group affected, ask five questions in order. Awareness: do they understand why the change is happening? Desire: do they want to take part? Knowledge: do they know how to change? Ability: can they actually do it in their daily work? And reinforcement: is there anything making it stick? Score each from one to five. The first low score is where to focus, because the later stages don't work without the earlier ones. Training people who don't see why is wasted effort. | ADKAR diagnostic<br>• Awareness: why?<br>• Desire: want to?<br>• Knowledge: how?<br>• Ability: can do?<br>• Reinforcement: sticks?<br>• Fix the first low score | Layout: five stepping stones across a stream labelled A, D, K, A, R. 0:00 a figure steps across; 0:20 stone D is missing and the figure stops; 0:30 a 'training' box appears beside stone K, labelled 'wasted if D is missing'; 0:45 stone D is placed and the figure continues. |
| 6 | 3:11 | 38 | Communicating change well is most of the work. Explain why, honestly, including what happens if you don't change. Say what changes and, just as important, what stays the same. Be honest about what you don't know yet, and when you'll know. Use two messengers: a senior sponsor to explain why, and people's direct manager to explain what it means for them, because people usually trust their own manager most. Communicate more than feels necessary, in several channels, and create ways for people to ask questions, including anonymously. | Communicating change<br>• Honest why<br>• What changes / what stays<br>• What we don't know yet<br>• Sponsor for why, manager for 'what it means for me'<br>• Repeat, several channels<br>• Questions welcome | Layout: a message plan graphic. 0:00 a sponsor icon broadcasts 'why'; 0:12 manager icons relay 'what it means for you' to small teams; 0:30 a two-column card 'changes / stays the same'; 0:45 an anonymous question box fills with notes. |
| 7 | 3:49 | 34 | A simple worked example. A small accounting practice in Leeds is moving from spreadsheets to cloud accounting software. <details><summary>Narration per scene (paste into ElevenLabs)</summary>

Scene 1 — Leading through change

```text
Think about the last big change at work: a new system, a restructure, a new way of working, perhaps AI tools arriving in your team. How did people react? Some were enthusiastic. Some were anxious. Some said nothing and quietly carried on the old way. That's normal. Change isn't just a project plan; it's an emotional experience for the people living through it. In this lecture, you'll learn models that help you understand change, how to diagnose where people are stuck with ADKAR, how to communicate change, support people through transition, and measure adoption.
```

Scene 2 — Why it matters

```text
Why does this matter? Because many change initiatives fail to deliver what they promised, and the reason is rarely the technology or the plan. It's that people don't adopt the change, or adopt it half-heartedly. A new system nobody uses properly is just an expensive system. Leaders who manage the human side of change get faster adoption, less disruption and keep people's trust for the next change. And there's always a next change.
```

Scene 3 — Models that help

```text
Here's the concept. Three models help. John Kotter's eight steps focus on leading organisational change: urgency, a guiding coalition, a vision, communication, removing obstacles, quick wins, building on change and anchoring it in culture. William Bridges' transition model says that change is the external event, but transition is the internal, psychological process: an ending, a neutral zone of confusion, then a new beginning. And Prosci's ADKAR model describes what each individual needs: awareness, desire, knowledge, ability and reinforcement. Here's the key idea: organisations don't change; people do, one at a time.
```

Scene 4 — Moving house

```text
Here's an analogy. Think of moving house. The move itself happens on one day: that's the change. But the transition takes months. There's the ending: leaving a home full of memories, even if the new one is better. Then the neutral zone: boxes everywhere, you can't find the kettle, your commute is unfamiliar. Then the new beginning: the new place finally feels like home. A good leader in change is like a good friend when you move house. They acknowledge the loss, help you through the boxes, and celebrate when you're settled.
```

Scene 5 — ADKAR diagnostic

```text
Now ADKAR as a diagnostic. For each group affected, ask five questions in order. Awareness: do they understand why the change is happening? Desire: do they want to take part? Knowledge: do they know how to change? Ability: can they actually do it in their daily work? And reinforcement: is there anything making it stick? Score each from one to five. The first low score is where to focus, because the later stages don't work without the earlier ones. Training people who don't see why is wasted effort.
```

Scene 6 — Communicating change

```text
Communicating change well is most of the work. Explain why, honestly, including what happens if you don't change. Say what changes and, just as important, what stays the same. Be honest about what you don't know yet, and when you'll know. Use two messengers: a senior sponsor to explain why, and people's direct manager to explain what it means for them, because people usually trust their own manager most. Communicate more than feels necessary, in several channels, and create ways for people to ask questions, including anonymously.
```

Scene 7 — Worked example 1: Helen's practice (illustrative)

```text
A simple worked example. A small accounting practice in Leeds is moving from spreadsheets to cloud accounting software. The partner, Helen, notices that the two most experienced bookkeepers keep using the old spreadsheets. She runs an ADKAR check. Awareness is fine; they know why. Desire is low: they're worried about looking slow in front of younger colleagues. So Helen asks them to co-design the new workflow and gives them extra practice time before go-live. They become the internal experts.
```

Scene 8 — Worked example 2: AI assistant rollout in Karachi (illustrative)

```text
Now a realistic business scenario, illustrative. A retail bank in Karachi is introducing an AI assistant to help branch staff answer customer questions. Rumours spread that it will replace jobs. The regional head, Ayesha, runs an ADKAR survey. Awareness is patchy and desire is low. She holds branch meetings where she explains why, what the tool will and won't do, how answers are checked, and that no roles are being cut in this phase. She trains branch champions, collects questions weekly, and publishes an updated FAQ. Adoption rises steadily over three months, and complaint handling times fall.
```

Scene 9 — Supporting transition

```text
Supporting people through transition means recognising the neutral zone. Productivity often dips temporarily. People feel uncertain and may grieve what they're losing, even if the change is good. So acknowledge the losses openly. Give extra time and help during the learning period. Create quick wins and share them. Use champions, respected peers who adopt early and help others. And look after yourself too. Leaders go through their own transition, often earlier than their team, and your team will notice your stress. Watch for change fatigue when too many changes arrive at once.
```

Scene 10 — Watch me: ADKAR + comms plan

```text
Watch me do it. I'm planning an illustrative change: moving my team's project tracking to a new tool. I fill in the ADKAR table for two groups. Project managers: high awareness, high desire, knowledge is the gap, so I plan hands-on training. Senior stakeholders: low awareness, so I brief them on why. Then I fill in the communication plan. The why: fewer missed deadlines. What stays the same: our weekly review. What we don't know yet: the date old data will be archived. I'll confirm by the end of the month. I post an anonymous question form link.
```

Scene 11 — Measuring adoption

```text
How do you measure adoption? Track usage of the new process or tool, error rates, time to complete key tasks, and support tickets. Watch for workarounds, like people keeping the old spreadsheet on the side. Run a short pulse question, such as: I understand why this change is happening and what it means for me. And check reinforcement after three and six months. Many changes succeed at launch and then slowly slide back. Celebrate progress and fix what's making the old way easier.
```

Scene 12 — Common mistakes

```text
Common mistakes. Announcing change only once, by email. Explaining what without why. Over-promising and hiding what you don't know. Training people before they want to change. Ignoring the emotional side and the losses. Declaring victory at launch. Too many changes at once. And introducing AI tools without explaining what they'll do, what data they use, and what it means for people's roles. Silence fills with rumours.
```

Scene 13 — Recap and try this now

```text
Let's recap. Change is emotional. Use Kotter for the organisation, Bridges for transition, and ADKAR to diagnose each group. Fix the first gap. Communicate honestly and often, with sponsors and direct managers. Support people through the neutral zone, with champions and quick wins, and measure real adoption over months. Your try this now: take a change you're leading or experiencing, fill in the ADKAR table and the communication plan from the lesson, and share one honest update with your team this week. Next, we'll look at leadership in the AI era, starting with teams that use AI agents.
```

</details>

<details><summary>Narration per scene (paste into ElevenLabs)</summary>

Scene 1 — Leading teams that work with AI agents

```text
Imagine this. It's Monday morning and your customer support team has a new colleague. It works twenty-four hours a day, handles hundreds of tickets, never gets tired, and occasionally, with total confidence, tells a customer something completely wrong. That colleague is an AI agent. More and more teams are working alongside them. The question for you as a leader isn't whether the technology is impressive. It's this: what work do we give it, with how much freedom, and who's accountable when it gets it wrong? In this lecture, you'll learn autonomy levels for agents, five leadership responsibilities, the governance basics, and how to write an agent charter.
```

Scene 2 — Why it matters

```text
Why does this matter now? Because agents are moving from demos into everyday work. They're built into mainstream office suites and customer-service platforms, and teams can build their own with automation tools. An assistant only suggests. An agent acts: it updates records, routes tickets, sends messages, runs a process. That's powerful, and it changes the leader's job. When an agent makes a mistake, it can repeat it hundreds of times before anyone notices. The management skills you already have, delegation, quality control and building trust, are exactly what make agents safe and useful.
```

Scene 3 — Autonomy levels for agents

```text
Here's the concept. Delegate to an agent the way you'd delegate to a new starter, with levels of autonomy. Level one, assist: the agent drafts or summarises, and a person decides and acts. Level two, recommend: the agent proposes an action with reasons, and a person approves each one. Level three, act with review: the agent acts, and people sample the results and can undo them. Level four, act within limits: the agent acts inside hard limits, such as a value cap, and people watch the metrics. Here's the key idea. Anything irreversible, customer-facing, legally significant, or about people's jobs and pay, stays at level one or two.
```

Scene 4 — The junior doctor

```text
Here's an analogy. Think of a hospital bringing in a brilliant new junior doctor. On day one, they don't operate alone. They take histories and suggest diagnoses, and a senior doctor signs off. As they show good judgement, they're trusted with more, but some decisions always need a consultant, and every patient has a named responsible clinician. Agents are like that junior doctor, but faster, more literal, and without the instinct to ask when something feels off. So you need the sign-offs, the named owner and the gradual trust even more.
```

Scene 5 — Five responsibilities

```text
Now five leadership responsibilities. One: a named human owner for every agent workflow, who defines the task, checks quality and answers for results. The agent can be responsible for a step. Only a person can be accountable. Two: clear boundaries, meaning what data the agent can see, what systems it can change, and what it must never do, with least-privilege access. Three: quality measurement, meaning sampling outputs and tracking errors, escalations, rework and complaints, not just volume. Four: psychological safety, so people feel safe saying the agent got this wrong. And five: skills and roles, planning training and talking honestly about how work will change.
```

Scene 6 — Psychological safety and AI errors

```text
Let's dwell on psychological safety, because it's where many teams quietly fail. Amy Edmondson's research on psychological safety describes teams where people feel able to speak up about mistakes and concerns without fear. With AI, there's an extra pressure: nobody wants to look like the person who's against innovation. So when the agent routes a ticket wrongly, people quietly fix it and move on. The error never gets logged and the agent never improves. Make reporting easy with a shared AI error log, and publicly thank the first person who uses it.
```

Scene 7 — Worked example 1: Sana in Lahore (illustrative)

```text
A simple worked example. Sana leads a six-person support team for an e-commerce brand in Lahore. The company introduces an agent that drafts replies and routes tickets. Sana sets refunds at level two: the agent recommends, a person approves every one. Routing goes to level three: the agent acts, and a team member reviews twenty routed tickets a day. She names Omar as the owner and starts an AI error log. After a month, routing accuracy is strong, so she moves routing to level four with limits. Refunds stay at level two. Money and trust aren't worth the risk.
```

Scene 8 — Worked example 2: Dubai agency (illustrative)

```text
Now a realistic business scenario, illustrative. A marketing agency in Dubai, with thirty staff, builds an agent that pulls campaign data every Monday, drafts client performance reports, and emails them. In week three, the agent sends a client a report containing another client's figures, because two data sources had similar names. The managing director, Rania, pauses the agent the same day and apologises to both clients. Then she fixes the system, not the blame. She writes an agent charter: a named owner per client report, no external emails without human approval, data access limited per client, and a weekly quality sample. The agent resumes at level two.
```

Scene 9 — Governance basics

```text
A word on governance. Keep it proportionate, but don't skip it. In the EU, the AI Act's AI literacy obligation has applied since the second of February, twenty twenty-five. Organisations that provide or deploy AI systems must take measures so staff who deal with them have sufficient AI literacy. Frameworks such as the NIST AI Risk Management Framework and the ISO standard for AI management systems give larger organisations a structured approach. And data protection law applies whenever agents touch personal data. So involve your data protection lead before connecting an agent to customer or employee records. This is general guidance, not legal advice.
```

Scene 10 — Watch me: an agent charter

```text
Watch me do it. I write an agent charter for an illustrative workflow: an agent that tags and summarises incoming sales enquiries. Purpose: route enquiries to the right account manager within an hour. Owner: our sales operations lead. Autonomy: level three, act with review. Allowed: read enquiries, add tags, write a summary. Never allowed: reply to customers or change deal values. Data: enquiry text only; our data protection lead has been consulted. Quality: sample fifteen a day; an error is a wrong tag or a missing key fact. Escalation: anyone can pause it from the team channel. Review monthly.
```

Scene 11 — Measuring success

```text
How do you measure whether it's working? Compare before and after on four things. Time saved. Quality: errors and, crucially, hidden rework, because time saved by the agent can reappear as time spent fixing its output. Customer outcomes, such as complaints and satisfaction. And team experience: do people trust it, and do they feel their work has become more or less meaningful? If time saved comes with rising complaints or hidden rework, the workflow isn't working yet. Adjust the autonomy level down, not just the prompt.
```

Scene 12 — Common mistakes

```text
Common mistakes. Giving an agent more freedom than you'd give a new starter. No named owner, so errors fall between teams. Measuring volume instead of quality. Punishing people who report AI mistakes. Connecting agents to personal or client data without a data protection review. Forgetting to tell customers when they need to know they're dealing with AI. And staying silent about what agents mean for people's roles, which fills the gap with rumours and resistance.
```

Scene 13 — Recap and try this now

```text
Let's recap. Delegate to agents like a fast, literal new starter, and match autonomy to risk, starting low. Keep irreversible, legal and people-related actions at assist or recommend. Give every workflow a named owner, clear boundaries, quality sampling and a pause button. Make it safe to report AI errors. And stay on top of AI literacy and data protection duties. Your try this now: list every AI assistant or agent your team uses and, for each, name the owner, the autonomy level and one quality metric. Then write a full charter for the riskiest one. Next, we'll master asynchronous communication.
```

</details>

<details><summary>Narration per scene (paste into ElevenLabs)</summary>

Scene 1 — Async communication that works

```text
It's four in the afternoon in Toronto, one in the morning in Karachi, and nine in the morning in Dubai. Your team needs to agree on a product change. If the only way to decide is a meeting, someone joins at dawn or at midnight, or the decision waits a week. There's a better way. In this lecture you'll learn what asynchronous communication really is, when to use it and when to meet, the five building blocks of an async team, how to write for readers you'll never be online with, and three templates you can use tomorrow.
```

Scene 2 — Why it matters

```text
Why does this matter? Async isn't a slogan about fewer meetings. It's a way of working that lets people across time zones, shift patterns and caring responsibilities contribute fully. It protects focus time, because people aren't pulled into calls every hour. And it creates a written record, so decisions don't get lost or re-argued. Some fully remote companies, GitLab being a well-known example with a public handbook, document an async-first approach in detail. But you don't need to be fully remote. Hybrid and office teams waste the same hours on status meetings and interruptions.
```

Scene 3 — Async or meet?

```text
Here's the concept. Async means people contribute at different times: written proposals, comments on documents, recorded video walkthroughs, threaded chat and written updates. Sync means real time: meetings, calls and live chat. Neither is better. They're tools for different jobs. Use async for status updates, proposals that need thoughtful input, reviewing documents, decisions with a clear owner and deadline, and walkthroughs. Meet for building relationships, conflict and sensitive topics, messy problems with many unknowns, and genuine crises. Here's the key idea: start async, and move to a meeting when the thread goes in circles or emotions rise. Three rounds, and we talk.
```

Scene 4 — The family recipe book

```text
Here's an analogy. Think of the difference between a phone call and a well-run relay of letters in a family recipe book. A phone call is great for catching up and sorting out a disagreement. But when your grandmother's recipe passes through the family, each person adds a note in the margin: less sugar, bake ten minutes longer. Nobody needs to be in the kitchen at the same time, and the book keeps the history. Async communication is the recipe book. It's slower for any single exchange, but the whole family contributes and nothing gets lost.
```

Scene 5 — Five building blocks

```text
Now the five building blocks. One: a written proposal format with a comment deadline, including owner, context, recommendation, options and a decide-by date. Two: channel norms and response times, such as chat the same working day, documents by the stated deadline, and phone only for real urgency. Three: one thread per topic, with a clear title, and no team decisions buried in direct messages. Four: recorded walkthroughs of three to five minutes, always with a written summary. And five: a decision log, a single line per decision: what, who, when, and a link to the thread. That's how async teams avoid re-arguing.
```

Scene 6 — Writing for async

```text
Writing for async is its own skill. Assume your reader wasn't in any of your meetings and is reading at a different time of day. Put the bottom line first. State exactly what you need, from whom, and by when, including the time zone. Give just enough context, and link to sources instead of writing as discussed. Say how to respond: agree, agree with changes, or object with a reason. And agree as a team whether silence by the deadline counts as no objection. Without that rule, proposals drift for weeks.
```

Scene 7 — Worked example 1: Maya's shop (illustrative)

```text
A simple worked example. Maya runs a small online shop in Bristol with two part-time staff who work different days. Things keep falling through the gaps because handovers happen by quick chats when they overlap, which is rarely. She sets up one shared thread per topic, suppliers, orders and social posts, and a short written handover at the end of each shift: done, in progress, needs attention. Orders stop being missed within a fortnight, and nobody has to stay late to catch the next person.
```

Scene 8 — Worked example 2: Farah's team (illustrative)

```text
Now a realistic business scenario, illustrative. Farah leads an eight-person product team split across Karachi, Dubai and Toronto, with only two hours of overlap. The daily stand-up suits Dubai, so Toronto joins at dawn and Karachi misses family dinners. Farah replaces it with an async stand-up in a thread by ten in the morning local time: yesterday, today, blockers. She keeps one forty-five-minute live meeting a week in the overlap, for problem-solving and connection. Proposals get forty-eight-hour comment windows. Blockers now surface earlier, because they're written down, and the team wins back several hours a week.
```

Scene 9 — AI and async

```text
Where does AI fit? AI features in chat and document tools can summarise long threads, pull out open questions, and draft decision-log entries. That's genuinely useful when you come back from leave to two hundred messages. But the summary can miss nuance or state a decision that wasn't actually made. So the decision owner checks it before it's recorded. And follow your team's rules on which tools can process internal information. Also watch for the opposite problem: AI makes it easy to write long messages. In async, shorter and clearer wins.
```

Scene 10 — Watch me: an async proposal

```text
Watch me do it. I write an illustrative async proposal: move our monthly client report from a slide deck to a live dashboard. Bottom line: I propose we switch from the March report. Context: the deck takes two days to build, and clients ask for data between reports. Options: switch, keep the deck, or a hybrid. Open question for our analyst: can the dashboard handle client-level permissions? Decide by Thursday, five in the afternoon, UK time. How to respond: agree, agree with changes, or object with a reason. Then I record a three-minute walkthrough of a prototype, paste the link and a written summary, and post it in the reporting thread.
```

Scene 11 — Measuring success

```text
How do you measure success? Track meeting hours per person and time to decision. Look at the share of decisions that make it into the decision log. Watch blocked-work time. And in pulse surveys, split by location: do people in every time zone feel they can contribute and are kept informed? If proposals routinely miss their deadlines, or the same decisions get re-opened, tighten your norms rather than adding meetings back.
```

Scene 12 — Common mistakes

```text
Common mistakes. Replacing meetings with a flood of unstructured messages. Proposals without deadlines. Expecting instant replies, which turns async into sync with extra steps. Videos with no written summary or no clear ask. Decisions made in private messages and never logged. Using async for conflict or sensitive feedback. And a leader who says async-first, then calls everyone into ad hoc meetings. People copy what you do, not what you write in the working agreement.
```

Scene 13 — Recap and try this now

```text
Let's recap. Async lets everyone contribute, protects focus and records decisions. Start async, and move to a meeting after three rounds or when emotions rise. Build the five blocks: proposals with deadlines, channel norms, topic threads, short walkthroughs with summaries, and a decision log. Write so nobody needs a follow-up call. Use AI summaries, but have owners check them. Your try this now: convert one recurring status meeting into an async stand-up or written update for two weeks, using the templates in the lesson, then ask the team what to keep. Next, we'll look at using AI in everyday management responsibly.
```

</details>

### 21. Using AI in everyday management, responsibly

Lesson `ai-in-everyday-management` · module “Leading in the AI era: agents, async and responsible AI” · target 9 min · 13 scenes · 1248 words · planned 9:02 (words ≈ 9:03) · status: **to produce**

It produces three beautifully written paragraphs in seconds. One of them mentions communication issues that never happened. The manager doesn't notice. The team member does. And from that moment, nothing that manager says about performance is fully trusted. AI can save managers hours every week. But its fluency is exactly what makes it risky. In this lecture you'll learn a traffic-light guide for AI in management, why the red zone matters, how to be transparent, how to protect data, and a safe drafting workflow. | Using AI in everyday management<br>• Traffic-light guide<br>• Why the red zone matters<br>• Transparency<br>• Protecting data<br>• A safe drafting workflow | Layout: a document titled 'Annual review' generates line by line in seconds. 0:10 one sentence 'communication issues' highlights red with a label 'never happened'; 0:22 a trust meter above an employee icon drops sharply; 0:35 agenda chips appear. |
| 2 | 0:47 | 37 | Why does this matter? Because managers are among the heaviest potential beneficiaries of AI, and among the most exposed to its risks. But management also involves decisions about people's careers, pay and wellbeing. Get those wrong, or let people believe a machine made them, and you damage fairness, you may break the law, and you lose trust that takes years to rebuild. | Why it matters<br>• Management = lots of writing and prep<br>• AI helps a great deal<br>• People decisions carry big risks<br>• Fairness, law, trust | Layout: a balance scale. 0:00 the left pan fills with 'time saved' icons: agenda, summary, draft; 0:20 the right pan fills with 'risk' icons: careers, pay, wellbeing; 0:35 three pillars beneath: fairness, law, trust. |
| 3 | 1:24 | 44 | Here's the concept: a traffic-light guide. Green: use freely, with checks. Summarising meetings, analysing anonymous survey comments, drafting feedback from your own notes, writing job descriptions, translation. Red: don't use AI to decide. Hiring, promotion, pay, termination, performance ratings, discipline, monitoring individuals, and anything involving health or grievances. Here's the key idea: red doesn't mean AI is banned from the building. It means a person makes the decision, through a proper process, with any tool approved and disclosed. | Traffic-light guide<br>• Green: drafting, summarising, prep, rehearsal<br>• Amber: meeting notes, survey themes, feedback drafts, JDs, translation<br>• Red: hiring, pay, ratings, discipline, monitoring<br>• Red = humans decide | Layout: a large traffic light. 0:00 green lamp lights and task cards slide beside it; 0:20 amber lamp lights with its cards and small shield icons; 0:40 red lamp lights with its cards and a person icon holding a gavel labelled 'humans decide'. |
| 4 | 2:08 | 40 | Here's an analogy. Think of a satnav and a driving licence. The satnav is brilliant. It plans routes, spots traffic and saves you time. But if it tells you to turn into a pedestrian street, you don't do it, and if you do, it's your licence, not the satnav's, on the line. AI is your management satnav. Follow it gladly on the motorway of routine drafting. Question it in unfamiliar streets. And never let it drive through the red lights of people decisions. The responsibility always stays with the person holding the licence. | Satnav and licence<br>• Great at routes and routine<br>• Question it in unfamiliar streets<br>• Never through red lights<br>• Your licence, your responsibility | Layout: a car dashboard with a satnav screen. 0:00 motorway route with a green tick; 0:12 a narrow pedestrian street with an instruction 'turn left' and a stop hand; 0:25 a red traffic light that the car waits at; 0:38 a driving licence card with a name field labelled 'you'. |
| 5 | 2:48 | 47 | Why is the red zone so important? Three reasons. Fairness: AI systems can reproduce biases from their training data or from how a prompt is framed, and a ranking or summary can quietly disadvantage a group. Law: data protection law, such as Article twenty-two of the UK and EU GDPR, restricts certain solely automated decisions with significant effects on people. And the EU AI Act lists AI used for recruitment, promotion, termination, task allocation and worker monitoring as high-risk, with obligations being phased in. Check the current timetable and your local law. And trust: people accept tough decisions more readily when they believe a fair human process was followed. | Why the red zone matters<br>• Fairness: bias in data and prompts<br>• Law: GDPR Article 22; EU AI Act high-risk employment uses<br>• Trust: fair human process<br>• Check current timetables and local law | Layout: three columns rise. 0:00 'Fairness' with a skewed ranking bar chart; 0:20 'Law' with two document icons labelled 'GDPR Art. 22' and 'EU AI Act: high-risk'; 0:40 'Trust' with two people shaking hands across a table; 0:55 a small caption 'check local law'. |
| 6 | 3:35 | 40 | Next, transparency and data. Tell your team how you use AI. A short statement works: I use our approved assistant to draft agendas and summarise documents. I don't use it to evaluate your performance. If you're unsure how AI was used in something that affects you, ask me. That one paragraph prevents the damaging moment when someone discovers hidden use. And protect data. Use only tools your organisation has approved for the data involved. Never paste health information, family circumstances, grievances or performance concerns into consumer tools. Anonymise: roles, not names. | Transparency and data<br>• Share a short AI use statement<br>• Invite questions<br>• Approved tools only<br>• No health, family, grievance or performance details<br>• Roles, not names | Layout: a statement card 'How I use AI as a manager' appears pinned in a team channel. 0:20 a padlock icon closes over a folder of personal data; 0:35 a name 'Ayesha K.' morphs into 'Senior analyst' in a prompt box. |
| 7 | 4:15 | 42 | A simple worked example. Asad runs a small accounting firm in Islamabad with seven staff. <details><summary>Narration per scene (paste into ElevenLabs)</summary>

Scene 1 — Using AI in everyday management

```text
Here's a scene that's playing out in offices everywhere. A manager, short of time, asks an AI assistant to write a team member's annual review. It produces three beautifully written paragraphs in seconds. One of them mentions communication issues that never happened. The manager doesn't notice. The team member does. And from that moment, nothing that manager says about performance is fully trusted. AI can save managers hours every week. But its fluency is exactly what makes it risky. In this lecture you'll learn a traffic-light guide for AI in management, why the red zone matters, how to be transparent, how to protect data, and a safe drafting workflow.
```

Scene 2 — Why it matters

```text
Why does this matter? Because managers are among the heaviest potential beneficiaries of AI, and among the most exposed to its risks. So much of management is writing, summarising and preparing: agendas, updates, one-to-one notes, feedback, plans. AI genuinely helps with all of that. But management also involves decisions about people's careers, pay and wellbeing. Get those wrong, or let people believe a machine made them, and you damage fairness, you may break the law, and you lose trust that takes years to rebuild.
```

Scene 3 — Traffic-light guide

```text
Here's the concept: a traffic-light guide. Green: use freely, with checks. Drafting routine messages and agendas, summarising long documents, preparing coaching questions, rehearsing a difficult conversation, structuring a plan. Amber: use with care and safeguards. Summarising meetings, analysing anonymous survey comments, drafting feedback from your own notes, writing job descriptions, translation. Red: don't use AI to decide. Hiring, promotion, pay, termination, performance ratings, discipline, monitoring individuals, and anything involving health or grievances. Here's the key idea: red doesn't mean AI is banned from the building. It means a person makes the decision, through a proper process, with any tool approved and disclosed.
```

Scene 4 — Satnav and licence

```text
Here's an analogy. Think of a satnav and a driving licence. The satnav is brilliant. It plans routes, spots traffic and saves you time. But if it tells you to turn into a pedestrian street, you don't do it, and if you do, it's your licence, not the satnav's, on the line. AI is your management satnav. Follow it gladly on the motorway of routine drafting. Question it in unfamiliar streets. And never let it drive through the red lights of people decisions. The responsibility always stays with the person holding the licence.
```

Scene 5 — Why the red zone matters

```text
Why is the red zone so important? Three reasons. Fairness: AI systems can reproduce biases from their training data or from how a prompt is framed, and a ranking or summary can quietly disadvantage a group. Law: data protection law, such as Article twenty-two of the UK and EU GDPR, restricts certain solely automated decisions with significant effects on people. And the EU AI Act lists AI used for recruitment, promotion, termination, task allocation and worker monitoring as high-risk, with obligations being phased in. Check the current timetable and your local law. And trust: people accept tough decisions more readily when they believe a fair human process was followed.
```

Scene 6 — Transparency and data

```text
Next, transparency and data. Tell your team how you use AI. A short statement works: I use our approved assistant to draft agendas and summarise documents. I don't use it to evaluate your performance. If you're unsure how AI was used in something that affects you, ask me. That one paragraph prevents the damaging moment when someone discovers hidden use. And protect data. Use only tools your organisation has approved for the data involved. Never paste health information, family circumstances, grievances or performance concerns into consumer tools. Anonymise: roles, not names.
```

Scene 7 — Worked example 1: Asad in Islamabad (illustrative)

```text
A simple worked example. Asad runs a small accounting firm in Islamabad with seven staff. He uses an approved assistant each Monday to turn his rough notes into a clear weekly plan and an agenda for the team meeting: green zone. When he wants to understand a staff survey, amber zone, he removes names and identifying details first, asks for themes with counts, then reads a random sample of raw comments himself. One theme the tool called workload stress turns out, in the actual comments, to be mostly about unclear priorities. He fixes priorities, not hours.
```

Scene 8 — Worked example 2: James in Manchester (illustrative)

```text
Now a realistic business scenario, illustrative. James manages a ten-person finance team in Manchester. Before year-end reviews, he uses the company's approved assistant to turn his own dated notes, kept throughout the year, into a first-draft structure for each review. He instructs it to use only the facts given. Then he checks every sentence against his notes. In one draft, he finds a sentence about communication issues that isn't in his notes at all. He deletes it. He rewrites openings in his own voice. The ratings are his, calibrated with other managers, with no AI involvement. Preparation time falls, and the reviews are more specific than last year's.
```

Scene 9 — Safe drafting workflow

```text
Let's make the safe drafting workflow explicit, because it applies to feedback, reviews and any sensitive message. Step one, write your own notes first: dated, specific, in situation, behaviour, impact form. Step two, anonymise. Step three, use an approved tool and tell it to use only the facts given, not to add examples, judgements or adjectives, and to flag anything vague or harsh. Step four, check every sentence against your notes and delete additions. Step five, rewrite the opening and closing in your own words. Step six, deliver it in a conversation, not as a pasted document.
```

Scene 10 — Watch me: AI use statement + audit

```text
Watch me do it. I write my personal AI use statement for an illustrative team. I use our approved assistant to draft agendas and routine messages, summarise long documents, prepare coaching questions and rehearse difficult conversations. I check every fact and rewrite in my own voice. I don't use AI to rate performance, decide pay, promotions or discipline, or to monitor individuals. I never paste personal, health or grievance information into AI tools. If you're unsure, ask me. Then I classify my last five uses. Four are green. One, summarising a team retrospective, is amber, and I realise I didn't tell the team. I'll mention it at the next meeting.
```

Scene 11 — Measuring success

```text
How do you know you're using AI well? Measure time saved on preparation. Look at how often you have to correct or retract something you sent. Ask in a pulse survey whether people feel AI is used fairly and transparently in the team. And aim for zero data incidents: nothing personal or confidential pasted into tools that shouldn't have it. If time saved is high but trust is falling, slow down. Trust is the harder thing to rebuild.
```

Scene 12 — Common mistakes

```text
Common mistakes. Sending AI drafts without checking facts and tone. Letting AI fill gaps in performance evidence with plausible sentences. Using consumer tools for personal or confidential information. Using AI secretly, and being found out. Treating an AI ranking or summary as objective, when it reflects its data and your prompt. Using AI to monitor individuals' activity or mood. And outsourcing empathy: an AI-written apology or thank-you that you don't personally mean.
```

Scene 13 — Recap and try this now

```text
Let's recap. AI can save managers hours, but its fluency hides errors, bias and the wrong tone. Use the traffic-light guide: green with checks, amber with safeguards, and red for people decisions that humans must make through proper process. Be transparent, protect data, and use the six-step safe drafting workflow. Your try this now: write your personal AI use statement using the template in the lesson, share it with your team, and classify your last five uses of AI by colour. That completes this course. Well done. Now pick one template from each module and use it this month.
```

</details>
